## 2026-08-12 (same day, follow-up): Enabled Full Scheduled Automation for Koku and Praxis (Live Ingestion + Nightly-Learn) — Templates Already Existed, Just Never Deployed; Found a Pre-Existing File-Watch Bug in Praxis's Live Mode Along the Way

**Trigger**: user noted koku/praxis had zero scheduled automation (flagged in
the same status-check that led to the RCA above) and asked to enable it.

**What was already there but undeployed**: `launchd/io.vectorize.cocoindex.koku.plist`,
`launchd/io.vectorize.cocoindex.praxis.plist` (live-mode ingestion,
`KeepAlive`+`RunAtLoad`, same pattern as kubernaut/engram) and
`launchd/io.vectorize.hindsight.nightly-koku.plist` all existed as
committed templates in the repo already — nobody had run the
`sed "s|__HOME__|$HOME|g" ... > ~/Library/LaunchAgents/...` + `launchctl
bootstrap` deploy step from `docs/NEW_PROJECT_SETUP.md` for them.
`nightly-praxis` had no template at all.

**Fix**: added `launchd/io.vectorize.hindsight.nightly-praxis.plist`
(mirrors nightly-koku, staggered to 03:30 to avoid colliding with koku's
03:00 and the old cleanup-once slot removed above), then deployed and
`launchctl bootstrap`ed all 4: `cocoindex.koku`, `cocoindex.praxis`,
`nightly-koku`, `nightly-praxis`. All loaded cleanly; koku's live
docs/code/issues apps started with zero errors.

**Found along the way — pre-existing bug, not caused by this change**:
praxis's live mode throws `RuntimeError: Cannot add watch ... - it is
already scheduled` (from `watchdog`'s macOS FSEvents backend) for the 4
`has_rust=True` repos (`praxis-forge`, `praxis-grid`, `praxis-operator`,
`praxis-policy`). Root cause: `docs_main` and `code_main` in
`flows/praxis-cocoindex-flows.py` each independently call
`localfs.walk_dir(repo_dir, recursive=True, ..., live=True)` on the *same*
`repo_dir` (both live off one shared `PRAXIS_ORG_DIR`, unlike kubernaut/
engram which deliberately point docs and code at *separate* pre-split
mirror directories specifically to avoid this — see 2026-08-03's
watch-mirrors entry). Two independent recursive FSEvents watches on the
identical path conflict; docs_main's watch (scheduled first) wins, code_main's
silently loses for those 4 repos. **Not fatal** — caught inside `watchdog`'s
own emitter thread, doesn't crash the process; issues/discussions/roadmap
polling and docs live-watching for all repos, plus code live-watching for
the non-Rust repos, all work correctly. **Net effect**: live *code* index
freshness for those 4 repos' `.rs` files is stale until the next manual
`--mode backfill` — everything else (docs, issues, the other repos' code)
is fully live. Documented as a known gap rather than patched under time
pressure; proper fix would mirror kubernaut's split-mirror pattern
(`watch-mirrors-config.sh`) for praxis, a bigger lift than this task's scope.

---

## 2026-08-12 (same day, follow-up): Added a `launchd` Watchdog for the Silent-Live-Service-Outage Failure Class

**Trigger**: user asked for instrumentation to catch this failure class
generally, on top of the specific RCA/fix above.

**What it does**: `cocoindex-watchdog.sh`
(`launchd/io.vectorize.cocoindex.watchdog.plist`, `StartInterval` 900s / 15
min) checks `launchctl list` for each of the 4 continuous live-mode services
(kubernaut, engram, koku, praxis — deliberately excludes dcm, which is
on-demand/backfill-only by design) and, if any is missing from the
`gui/$UID` domain, re-`bootstrap`s it and logs the intervention with a
timestamp to `~/.hindsight/logs/cocoindex-watchdog.log`. Logs a quiet
one-line heartbeat when nothing needs fixing, so the log stays skimmable
while still proving the watchdog itself is alive. Deployed and verified:
first run correctly reported all 4 services present.

This turns any future occurrence of this failure class (this specific root
cause is fixed, but the watchdog is generic insurance against the next
one) from a silent multi-hour-to-multi-day outage into a same-15-minute-cycle
auto-heal with an audit trail.

---

## 2026-08-09 (same day, follow-up): First Fix Landed `cocoindex_search` Guidance in 3 GitHub PRs — Reverted After User Caught It's a Personal-Only Tool, Replaced With a `.git/info/exclude`-Protected Local Symlink

**Trigger**: immediately after the fix below shipped 3 PRs, the user pointed
out: "cocoindex is specific to our local env, any other contributor without
engram installed will struggle. I think these changes should be local and
not tracked in github because of that."

**Correct call, missed in the original fix**: `cocoindex_search` (the
`cocoindex-code` MCP server) only works because it's backed by *this
machine's* `~/.hindsight` Postgres instance and Python venv
(`~/.hindsight/cocoindex-search.py`). Any other contributor cloning
`kubernaut`/`kubernaut-operator`/`kubernaut-console` without an Engram
install would see `AGENTS.md` recommend a tool that doesn't exist for them.
`gopls`, by contrast, was correctly left alone in all 3 PRs — it's a public
Go toolchain binary anyone can `go install`, and `AGENTS.md` already
documented it that way ("For other MCP-compatible agents... add `gopls mcp`
to your MCP server configuration"). The mistake was treating both MCP tools
as equally shareable when only one actually is.

**Fix**: closed all 3 PRs
([kubernaut#2031](https://github.com/jordigilh/kubernaut/pull/2031),
[kubernaut-operator#317](https://github.com/jordigilh/kubernaut-operator/pull/317),
[kubernaut-console#75](https://github.com/jordigilh/kubernaut-console/pull/75))
— every line changed in them was `cocoindex_search`-related, so there was
nothing left to salvage once that's removed. Replaced with a fully local
mechanism, mirroring the existing `.cursor/mcp.json` symlink-from-template
pattern:

- New shared template:
 `~/.hindsight/cursor-rule-templates/local-mcp-tool-preference.mdc`
 (`alwaysApply: true`), whose own header explicitly says not to promote its
 content into the repo's shared docs.
- Extended `post-checkout-cursor-mcp.sh` to symlink this template into
 `.cursor/rules/local-mcp-tool-preference.mdc` in every kubernaut-family
 clone, **and** to add a matching `.git/info/exclude` line (per-repo,
 resolved via `git rev-parse --git-path info/exclude` so it works
 correctly from worktrees too) — so it's protected from an accidental
 `git add -A` by a real git mechanism, not just by remembering not to
 stage it.
- Backfilled by running the hook once across all 5 target repos (`kubernaut`,
 `kubernaut-v1.5`, `kubernaut-v1.6`, `kubernaut-operator`,
 `kubernaut-console`); verified the symlink resolves and `git status` shows
 nothing in all 5.
- Caught and fixed an unrelated bug while wiring this up:
 `kubernaut-console`'s `core.hooksPath` was a stale absolute path
 (`kubernaut-demo-console/.githooks`) left over from a repo rename — the
 directory no longer existed, so **all** of that repo's hooks, including
 its pre-commit sensitive-data-detection hook, had been silently not
 firing. Repointed to the relative `.githooks` and installed the
 `post-checkout` hook there for the first time (it previously had none).

**Bug in the hook code itself, caught before it shipped**: the first
version of the `post-checkout` addition placed the new symlink/exclude
logic *after* the existing `mcp.json` block's early-`exit 0` checks — which
fire in the common case (symlink already correct), so the new logic never
ran on any of the 4 repos that already had a correct `mcp.json` symlink.
Manually backfilling immediately after writing the hook surfaced this
(4 of 5 repos silently didn't get the file); moved the new block before the
early exits fixed it for all 5.

**Process note**: this is the second time in two consecutive turns that an
immediate, hands-on verification step (running the actual command / editing
the actual file rather than trusting the diff) caught a real bug before it
became a silent gap -- the `NOT EXCLUDED` output for 4/5 repos would have
gone unnoticed without it.

---

## 2026-08-09: "Use MCP Tools, Not grep" Kept Recurring in kubernaut Because `AGENTS.md` Documented `gopls` But Never Mentioned `cocoindex_search` — Root Cause Was a Doc Gap, Not (Only) Non-Compliance

**Correction (see the follow-up entry above)**: the fix described below shipped
`cocoindex_search` guidance into 3 GitHub PRs against shared, committed files.
That was wrong — `cocoindex_search` only works on machines with a personal
Engram/Hindsight install, unlike `gopls` (a public, `go install`-able binary).
All 3 PRs were closed and replaced with a local-only, git-excluded symlink.
The diagnosis in this entry (the doc gap itself) still stands; only the *where
to put the fix* was wrong.

**Trigger**: user asked to update kubernaut's `AGENTS.md` and memory banks to make
the model prioritize MCP tools over `grep`/`rg`. Recall surfaced this exact
correction recurring at least twice already (2026-08-03, 2026-08-04) despite
generic "prefer MCP" guidance already existing in this session's own
workspace rules — which looked at first like the same "model ignores
AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md at will" pattern already documented for DCM's
`CLAUDE.md` checklist (see the 2026-08-08 hook-delivered-checklist entries).

**What investigation actually found, in order of surprise**:

1. Kubernaut has no `AGENTS.md` locally on `main`'s tree either — wait, it
 does, but the local `kubernaut` and `kubernaut-v1.5` clones were checked
 out on feature branches **1663 and 1692 commits behind `origin/main`**
 respectively, predating `AGENTS.md`'s creation (2026-06-28) entirely. Their
 working trees had no `AGENTS.md` at all — not a content problem, an
 out-of-date-branch problem, same shape as the `gopls`-stale-index bug from
 the prior entry.
2. `kubernaut-v1.6` and `kubernaut-operator` *did* have `AGENTS.md` locally
 (12 and 40 commits behind `main` — close enough to have it), and it
 **already contained** a `gopls MCP > gopls CLI > grep` preference
 hierarchy with concrete MCP usage examples (`go_symbol_references`,
 `go_search`) — this had been present since the file's creation.
3. But `AGENTS.md` never mentioned `cocoindex_search` anywhere, in any
 kubernaut-family repo. For an exact-symbol Go lookup, `gopls` was
 correctly documented and (per the file) had been all along. For a
 semantic/conceptual search — "how does X work", "where do we handle Y" —
 there was no MCP-preferred path documented at all, so the fallback was
 grep by default. The recurring correction was very likely hitting this
 exact gap, not necessarily the model ignoring guidance that existed.
4. The `.cursor/rules/*.mdc` files that looked grep-saturated (~40 `grep -r`
 examples prescribed as verification methodology across 9 files) turned
 out to be from the same stale branches. `origin/main` had already
 consolidated that whole rule system down to `AGENTS.md` as "the single
 authoritative source" (per its own header), leaving only 8 grep instances
 across 3 glob-scoped files — most of which were legitimate (parsing
 `go build` output, literal anti-pattern string checks with no MCP
 equivalent).
5. Found and fixed a genuinely separate bug while in there:
 `kubernaut-operator`'s `.cursor/mcp.json` was a **plain file**, not the
 usual machine-local symlink to the shared template, and it matched the
 "no-Go" template even though the repo has a `go.mod` — so `gopls` wasn't
 wired up at all in Cursor, despite `AGENTS.md` claiming it was
 "pre-configured." Converted it to a symlink to `kubernaut-family-go.json`.

**Fix**: opened PRs against `origin/main` (never against the stale local
branches) for all 3 affected repos, adding a `cocoindex_search` MCP
subsection alongside the existing `gopls` one, and updating the handful of
still-grep-based discovery steps that had a real MCP equivalent:

- [jordigilh/kubernaut#2031](https://github.com/jordigilh/kubernaut/pull/2031)
 — `AGENTS.md` + 3 rule files (`03-testing-strategy.mdc`, `04-ai-ml.mdc`,
 `14-design-decisions-documentation.mdc`)
- [jordigilh/kubernaut-operator#317](https://github.com/jordigilh/kubernaut-operator/pull/317)
 — `AGENTS.md` only (no grep-heavy rule files there)
- [jordigilh/kubernaut-console#75](https://github.com/jordigilh/kubernaut-console/pull/75)
 — new `AGENTS.md` (the repo had none at all); no `gopls` section since it's
 TypeScript/React with no Go, just `cocoindex_search` vs. grep

Left build-log parsing and literal anti-pattern greps (e.g.
`grep "undefined:\|cannot use"`, `grep -r "os.getenv"`) untouched — no MCP
tool replaces those.

**Decision on hook-based enforcement**: given the root cause looks more like
an actual, fixable content gap than pure model non-compliance (unlike the
DCM `CLAUDE.md` checklist case), decided to hold off building a
`preToolUse` Grep-nudge hook for now and see whether the correction stops
recurring once these 3 PRs merge, before adding more hook infrastructure.

---

## 2026-08-08 (same day, second follow-up): Narrowed the 8 Leftover Entries Into Specific Questions — 4 Confirmed by the User, 3 Resolved by Codebase/Cluster Investigation, 1 Reversed the Expected Direction

**Trigger**: direct continuation of the prior entry. Rather than re-asking the
8 mis-clustered entries at the cluster-title level again, each was rewritten
into its actual specific dispute (with the real quoted transcript text) and
posed as 8 independent multiple-choice questions.

**5 the user answered directly** from memory: Kind's canonical local-load
pattern is `podman save | kind load` (not push-then-pull); Kind nodes *can*
reach quay.io directly (manually-loaded images should be removed so kubelet
pulls cleanly); koku amd64 builds require `helios08` or CI, never the local
host; the "kubectl for bulk YAML + `test/infrastructure/`" claim isn't a
single rule — it's Helm for v1.6 E2E FP/fleet deployments (validates the
chart) vs. programmatic Go elsewhere (which can itself invoke Helm as a
library, e.g. Envoy AI Gateway); and the E2E FP AAP/AWX scenario really was
dropped for resource constraints — E2E WE runs the AAP scenario instead.

**3 the user explicitly deferred to investigation** rather than guessing from
memory — "check the documentation and code," "check against source and
hindsight and reassess," "check on the dev cluster and reassess" — which
matters methodologically: the user modeled exactly the behavior this whole
correction-reduction effort is trying to instill (verify against ground
truth instead of asserting from recollection), on the exact three questions
where recollection was weakest.

- **`af_` vs `kubernaut_` tool prefix** (`cocoindex_search` + grep on
  `kubernaut`): `pkg/apifrontend/handler/mcptools.go` registers 23 tools,
  100% `kubernaut_`-prefixed, zero `af_`. Git history shows a deliberate
  removal (issue #1230: "replace af_* triage tools with kubectl_*"), and
  `ADR-022-af-sa-unified-security-model.md` explicitly **rejects** the
  user-scoped(af_)/SA-scoped(kubernaut_) split the old memory described —
  all AF K8s calls now use one ServiceAccount regardless of entry point.
  Remaining `af_` strings (file names, Prometheus metrics, internal state
  keys) are legacy artifacts, not tool names.
- **Does `001_v1_schema.sql` seed `action_type_taxonomy`?** Confirmed
  schema-only (zero `INSERT`) and byte-identical across `kubernaut`,
  `kubernaut-v1.5`, and `kubernaut-v1.6`. The disputed "seeds 10/25 directly"
  claim traced to now-archived pre-consolidation migrations
  (`migrations/v0-archived/025_action_type_taxonomy.sql`, superseded). Live
  seeding path: CRD → AuthWebhook → `pkg/datastorage/repository/actiontype/crud.go`'s
  single remaining `INSERT`. Bonus finding not previously in memory at all:
  on true upstream `main`, migration `016_drop_workflow_catalog_and_action_type_tables.sql`
  (issue #1661 Phase G) drops `action_type_taxonomy` from Postgres
  **entirely** — etcd/CRDs are sole source of truth, DataStorage's read path
  is now a pure informer cache with zero SQL queries against that table. The
  local `kubernaut` and `kubernaut-v1.5` clones don't have this migration
  (both behind true `origin/main`), so the discrepancy was partly a stale-clone
  artifact, not just a knowledge gap.
- **PrometheusRule restart threshold, checked live on the connected dev
  cluster** (`kubectl get prometheusrules -A`): no rule anywhere currently
  uses the disputed `increase(kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total[5m]) > 2`
  expression. `demo-controllers-rules` uses `rate(...)×60×5 > 0` (functionally
  "> 0 restarts", matching the existing memory's claimed fix); `console-e2e-alerts`
  and `demo-app-alerts-checkout` skip restart-counting entirely in favor of
  `max_over_time(kube_pod_container_status_waiting_reason{reason="CrashLoopBackOff"}) >= 1`
  with `for: 30s` — a more direct, faster-firing signal. The old ">2"
  observation was likely accurate for its moment but has since been
  superseded by this redesign, not merely a threshold tweak.

**1 came back reversed**: the koku amd64-build entry's `new_statement`
("you can build here as well") turned out to be the *wrong* side once the
user confirmed helios08/CI-only — the original existing memory was right all
along. This is the first entry across both passes where investigation
disproved the newer statement instead of the older one, a useful reminder
that "new_statement" chronological ordering carries no epistemic weight by
itself.

Executed via `/tmp/execute_followup_8.py` (same `invalidate_retain`/`remove_only`
shape, `--dry-run` default). Backed up the queue to
`/tmp/contradictions-pending.jsonl.bak-followup-8` first. Result: **3
`invalidate_retain`, 5 `remove_only`, 4 standalone retains** (koku build-host
rule, the main-branch Postgres-table-drop finding, the Helm-vs-Go
context-dependent rule, and the E2E FP/WE AAP scope). 2/3 `invalidate_memory`
calls returned the same benign "already gone" 404 — 0 retain failures.
Queue: **48 → 40**. Regenerated `docs/DASHBOARD.md`/`docs/PENDING_CONTRADICTIONS.md`.

**Takeaway**: narrowing a mis-clustered entry down to its literal dispute
(with the actual quoted transcript lines) rather than re-asking at the
cluster-title level did two things a cluster-level re-ask couldn't have: it
let the user correctly say "I don't know, go check" on exactly the 3
questions where a cluster-level framing would have tempted a confident-sounding
guess, and it surfaced one case (koku amd64) where the *original* memory,
not the newer statement, turned out to be right — something a "trust the
correction, it's usually the fix" heuristic would have gotten backwards.

## 2026-08-08 (same day, follow-up): Resolved the 13 Judgment-Call Clusters With User-Provided Ground Truth — 72 → 48, and Found the Resolution Wasn't 1:1 Per Cluster

**Trigger**: direct continuation of the bulk-triage entry below. The
`escalated-contradictions-synthesis` canvas had surfaced 13 "judgment-call"
clusters (28 entries) that needed domain knowledge only the user has, plus 8
"bulk-approve candidate" and other clusters already resolved in the prior
pass. The user answered all 13 clusters' synthesized questions across three
messages (10, then 3 more), covering: image registry policy (quay vs GHCR),
AF's role calling KA, Action Type storage versioning (Postgres vs etcd),
Kessel/SpiceDB ("drop this, no longer relevant"), Helm vs. programmatic Go for
E2E, DEX vs. Keycloak, OPA/Rego usage since inception, PrometheusRule/ArgoCD
reconciliation timing, E2E timing assertions (`Eventually()` with a max
timeout), test-skip policy ("no skips allowed"), FMC image build/CI policy,
notification channel support (Slack/file/console/Teams/PagerDuty, no email),
and LLM remediation-history context (implemented since v1.0).

**What actually happened when the answers were checked against each entry's
own text, not just the cluster title**: the 13 clusters covered 32 unique
entries (one, `d94d7c29`, double-counted across two clusters), and the
user's answer resolved each cluster's *titular* question cleanly — but 8 of
the 32 entries turned out to be about a **different, more specific dispute**
that the clustering pass had merged in because the transcript excerpts shared
vocabulary (e.g. "image", "build", "AWX/Ansible") with the entries the
cluster title actually describes. Applying a cluster-level answer to those 8
would have been a guess dressed up as ground truth, so they were left `leave`d
(untouched, still in the queue) — e.g. two "Image Registry" entries about
whether Kind nodes can reach `quay.io` over the network, and whether to
`podman save | kind load` vs. push-and-pull, are not resolved by "quay.io is
for releases, GHCR is for PR/CI-run images."

A second, separate class showed up too: **9 of the 32 entries were
self-evidently resolvable from their own already-recorded `[CORRECTION]`
text**, independent of anything the user said today (e.g. an in-session
"you don't build here, you push to GitHub and use the build image job there"
directly supersedes an earlier "build locally" instruction in the same
transcript) — the user's cluster answer served as confirmation/context, not
the sole evidence. And a third class (11 entries) turned out, on close
reading, **not to be real contradictions at all** once the missing nuance was
supplied — e.g. "quay.io for the v1.5.2 release" and "GHCR for PR builds" are
both true simultaneously (different image *purpose*, not a factual dispute),
and "SP outputs labels via Rego" / "no need to modify the Rego policy for
this specific case" are compatible, not contradictory. These were
`remove_only` (cleared from the queue, no invalidate needed since nothing was
actually wrong).

Final per-entry breakdown across the 32 entries: **13 `invalidate_retain`**
(old memory confirmed wrong/stale, corrected statement retained), **11
`remove_only`** (not a real contradiction — cleared without touching any
existing memory), **8 `leave`** (cluster answer didn't address that specific
entry's actual dispute — still pending, needs a narrower follow-up). Also
retained **2 standalone policy statements** not tied to any single
invalidate (the quay/GHCR rule, and the Postgres-v1.5-vs-etcd-v1.6+ storage
split) since no single existing memory was "the wrong one" to invalidate for
those — the gap was a missing general fact, not a stale one.

Executed via `/tmp/execute_judgment_clusters.py` (`--dry-run` default,
`--execute` flag; not checked in), same `invalidate_memory` +
`hindsight_retain` + `remove_pending` shape as the prior triage script, keyed
by hand-verified UUIDs (re-fetched from `/tmp/escalated-clusters-fixed.json`
+ the live queue immediately before running, specifically *because* several
UUIDs referenced in this same conversation had scrolled out of context — see
takeaway below). Backed up the queue to
`/tmp/contradictions-pending.jsonl.bak-judgment-clusters` first. Result:
**5 of the 13 `invalidate_memory` calls returned `False`** (same documented
"already gone"/404 contract as the prior pass — all 5 had `retained: true`
regardless, so no information was lost), **0 retain failures**. Queue: **72 →
48** entries. Regenerated `docs/DASHBOARD.md` / `docs/PENDING_CONTRADICTIONS.md`.

**Takeaways**:
- **A synthesized cluster question is a hypothesis about what's actually in
  dispute, not a guarantee** — verify it against each member entry's own
  text before applying the answer to all of them. 8/32 entries here (25%)
  were mis-clustered by shared vocabulary rather than shared dispute; treating
  the cluster title as ground truth for every member would have silently
  written 8 fabricated "corrections" into the memory bank.
- **Some entries resolve themselves from already-recorded evidence and don't
  need new input at all** — 9/32 had an explicit `[CORRECTION]` already in
  the transcript excerpt that settled the question on its own. The user's
  answer was corroborating context for these, not the deciding evidence;
  worth distinguishing "the user just told me X" from "the transcript already
  proved X" when writing the retained statement's justification.
- **"Flagged as contradictory" and "actually in conflict" are not the same
  claim** — 11/32 (34%) turned out to be two true statements about different
  scopes (release vs. PR image, one CRD kind vs. another, a general policy vs.
  a specific case) that a weak semantic match conflated. This is now the
  single largest resolution category across both passes combined (11 here +
  63 auto_reject in the prior pass = 74 total confirmed non-contradictions
  out of 219 original entries, 34%), reinforcing that the contradiction
  *detection* side (not just the backlog-clearing side) has real precision
  headroom — see GitHub issue #13.
- **Don't trust remembered UUIDs across a long session, even your own** —
  re-derived all 32 entry ids from the source files immediately before
  executing rather than relying on the 8-character prefixes carried in
  reasoning/analysis earlier in the same conversation; a dry-run count match
  (32/32 found, 0 missing) confirmed they were exact, but the check was only
  possible because the plan was built as data (a dict keyed by full UUID)
  and validated against the live queue before any mutation, not because the
  UUIDs were assumed correct from memory.

---

## 2026-08-08: Bulk-Triaged and Cleared the Pending Contradictions Backlog — 219 → 72 via LLM-Assisted Classification, Semantic Clustering, and a Scripted Execution Pass

**Trigger**: a status-report follow-up. After explaining that the 218 pending
contradictions were already the human-review queue (low-confidence
supersession candidates the three-tier check couldn't auto-resolve, per
`contradiction_resolution.py`), the user asked "is it something I need to
address one by one or can it be automated via LLM?", then, after a dry-run
classification pass, "triage the 90 to escalate for duplicated or related
that can further reduce the numbers" / "or synthetize them so that I can
address them in bulk", and finally chose to execute the result end-to-end
("execute_all ... Recommended") rather than stop at another dry run.

**Pass 1 — bulk triage (all 219 entries)**: a one-off script
(`/tmp/triage_pending_contradictions.py`, not checked in) sent each entry's
existing memory + new statement + original contradiction-check explanation to
Sonnet (`vertex_ai/claude-sonnet-4-6`, 10 parallel workers, 73s wall time) and
asked it to bucket into `auto_approve` (new statement genuinely supersedes
the existing memory), `auto_reject` (not a real contradiction — restatement,
weak match, or the existing memory is still true in a different scope), or
`escalate` (genuinely ambiguous, needs a human), defaulting to escalate on
uncertainty. Result: **66 auto_approve, 63 auto_reject, 90 escalate** — a 59%
reduction in items needing real judgment on its own. Presented to the user
as a Cursor canvas (`pending-contradictions-triage.canvas.tsx`; canvases live
outside the repo, under `~/.cursor/projects/<workspace>/canvases/`, not
checked in here).

**Pass 2 — clustering the 90 escalate entries**: a second script
(`/tmp/cluster_escalated.py`) sent all 90 escalate entries in a single call
(same model, `max_tokens=8000`, needed a 240s timeout — an 8000-token
structured-JSON generation over ~90 grouped entries routinely ran 150-190s,
well past litellm's default assumptions) and asked it to group entries
sharing the same underlying disputed fact into one cluster with one
synthesized decision question, plus a `suggested` default where the evidence
clearly leaned one way. Result: **38 clusters + 26 true singletons = 64
decision units** (down from 90), including 17 clusters that genuinely merge
2-6 entries into one question (e.g. "Branch/Worktree Identity Corrections"
merged 5 separate entries about which branch/worktree was current at
different points in the session — the same session-relative-state confusion
recurring, not 5 independent facts). Coverage-checked programmatically (every
input id must appear in exactly one output group): the model's own output had
**two ID typos** (a dropped hyphen and a transposed digit) that mapped
cleanly onto two real entries once corrected — worth noting since it means
"the LLM's structured-output ID list is self-consistent" is not implied by
"the LLM says it covered everything" and needs an explicit set-equality check
against the real input ids, not just eyeballing the counts.

**Execution — mirroring `review-contradictions.py`'s existing approve/reject
code path exactly, just driven by the triage buckets instead of an
interactive per-entry prompt.** A third script
(`/tmp/execute_triage.py`, `--dry-run` default / `--execute` flag) built an
`{entry_id: action}` map:
- `auto_approve` (66) → `invalidate_retain`: `invalidate_memory("cursor-memory", memory_id, reason=...)` (soft-retire, reversible) then `hindsight_retain(..., tags=["CORRECTION", "supersedes-prior-memory"])`, then `remove_pending(id)`.
- `auto_reject` (63) → `remove_only`: just `remove_pending(id)`, no invalidate, no retain.
- Of the 90 escalate entries, only the **21 entries inside the 11 clusters that had a `suggested` default** were touched at all — and even then, not uniformly. Reading each cluster's `suggested` text before wiring an action revealed three different shapes, not one:
  - **14 entries** (5 clusters: Branch/Worktree Identity, snake_case-vs-camelCase, kubernaut-read-only-vs-explicit-edit, README roadmap, Gateway/Redis) had a clean "new supersedes old" lean → `invalidate_retain`.
  - **4 entries** (3 clusters: IQE test-run discrepancy, sizeLimit-on-emptyDir, Keycloak/Kind user count) had a "not really a contradiction, both can be true" lean → `remove_only`, no invalidate.
  - **3 entries** (dsBearerTokenFile, HAPI `from src.` import convention, tool cluster-name prefixing) had a *suggested* field that was actually a deferral in disguise — "verify git history to confirm," "a human needs to decide" — not a directive. These were deliberately left `leave`d (untouched, still in the queue) despite technically having a non-null `suggested` string, because the string's content wasn't actually a safe default to auto-execute. This is the same trap as Pass 2's ID-typo issue: a field being *present* isn't the same as the field's content being *actionable*, and both needed a second look rather than blind execution.
  - The remaining 69 escalate entries (no cluster, or a cluster with no `suggested` default) were left untouched.

Backed up `contradictions-pending.jsonl` to
`~/.hindsight/logs/archive/contradictions-pending-backup-2026-08-08-pre-triage.jsonl`
before running with `--execute`. Final result: **80 invalidate_retain + 67
remove_only + 72 leave = 219**, queue file now **72 entries** (kubernaut 66,
koku 4, dcm 2). All 80 `hindsight_retain` calls succeeded (0 retain
failures). 22 of the 80 `invalidate_memory` calls returned `False` (confirmed
via direct `GET .../memories/{id}` → `404` on samples) — not a bug, the
documented "already gone" contract in `contradiction_resolution.py`; most
likely explanation is that the underlying memory had already been
consolidated or auto-resolved through an unrelated, higher-confidence
contradiction sometime between when the entry was queued (many dated back to
July 31/Aug 1) and today. The corrective statement was retained in all 80
cases regardless, so no information was lost — worst case, an already-gone
memory was targeted for a no-op invalidate. Regenerated
`docs/DASHBOARD.md`/`docs/PENDING_CONTRADICTIONS.md` via
`generate-dashboard.py` immediately after to reflect 72 pending (was 218/219).

**Takeaways**:
- **Semantic clustering finds a different, larger class of redundancy than
  mechanical dedup.** A direct duplicate-memory-id / duplicate-statement-text
  check on the same 90 entries found only 2 exact memory-id repeats and 2
  near-duplicate statement prefixes — LLM clustering by underlying disputed
  fact found 17 genuine multi-entry clusters (47 entries). The two techniques
  are complementary, not substitutes; relying on mechanical dedup alone would
  have missed the real reduction opportunity entirely.
- **A backlog can look uniformly "needs a human" and still contain three
  different actionability tiers once you actually read the model's reasoning
  text**: clean directive, "not actually a contradiction" (also a directive,
  just a different one), and "I can't tell without checking the live repo" —
  auto-executing the last tier as if it were the first would have been the
  same category of mistake as the FMC-HTTP-API false-positive block (GitHub
  issue #13) that started this whole investigation: trusting stale/unverified
  text as a bulk-action signal instead of stopping at "needs a human with
  access to current ground truth."
- **When asking an LLM for structured output where every input id must map
  to exactly one output group, verify that with a real set-difference check,
  not a rendered count.** The 90-in/90-out counts matched even with two
  hallucinated ids in the raw output, because a different real id was
  coincidentally still missing by the same count — coverage-checking by
  count alone would have silently shipped two wrong invalidate/retain
  actions (or, worse, silently dropped two entries from the backlog with no
  record either way).
- Confirms the standing GitHub issue #13 hypothesis empirically at a larger
  scale: 66/219 (30%) of the backlog were genuine stale-fact supersessions
  the automated check *should* have been confident enough to auto-resolve on
  its own, and another 63/219 (29%) were false contradictions the check
  should not have queued at all — both are exactly the "automatic
  supersession detection" gap the issue proposes addressing structurally,
  not just clearing manually every so often.

## 2026-08-07 (same day, fifth follow-up): `CodePattern` Verdict Reversed From "Skip" to "Add" — Implemented `*_code_pattern_search` MCP Tools Across All Four Projects, Confirmed It Replaces Neither `gopls` Nor Serena

**Trigger**: direct continuation of the earlier same-day `CodePattern` spike
(previous entry), whose final verdict was "skip — redundant with
already-adopted LSP tooling" but explicitly left the door open: "worth
reopening only if a concrete query shape emerges that neither `gopls` nor
Serena can answer." User reopened it by asking "I think we have se[re]na for
this already. Thoughts?", then "gopls is fine, but it adds another MCP, so
consolidating it to cocoindex would help" (a genuine distinct angle: not "can
LSP already do this query" but "can this retire an extra MCP server"), then
after a pros/cons comparison: "ok let's add it then. Would this replace
serena for koku instead?"

**Answer to the reopened question: no — `CodePattern` replaces neither
`gopls` nor Serena, for the identical reason in both cases.** Serena
(onboarded 2026-07-31 as koku's `gopls`-equivalent) is Pyright-backed: real
`find_symbol`, `find_referencing_symbols`, symbol-level edits, and
diagnostics — type-aware, cross-file semantic capability. `CodePattern` is
purely syntactic tree-sitter matching: no type resolution (won't unify
`(bool, error)` with `(ok bool, err error)`), no find-references, no call
graph, no diagnostics. The "consolidate the MCP" framing doesn't change this
calculus — the two tools solve different problems (shape search vs.
symbol-graph navigation) regardless of how many servers they're bundled
into; retiring Serena/gopls in favor of `CodePattern` would be a real
capability loss, not a consolidation. `CodePattern`'s actual, narrower win is
retiring the *"stand up a whole extra MCP server just for one shape-search
capability"* problem specifically for languages that don't already have an
LSP tool in place — which is nobody in this repo's current project set
(kubernaut/dcm/engram have `gopls`, koku has Serena) — so its marginal value
here is exactly the shape-search queries neither LSP tool can answer, same
conclusion as the original spike, just now shipped instead of deferred.

**Implementation** (TDD, RED→GREEN for the one pure-logic unit; the four
search scripts follow this repo's existing convention of no unit tests for
DB/MCP-wrapper code — none of `search_code`/`_rrf_fuse`/`_format_results`
have tests either):

- **`chunking.find_code_files(root, included_patterns, excluded_patterns)`**
  — new shared helper, `tests/test_chunking.py::TestFindCodeFiles` (5 cases:
  include-only, include+exclude, no-match, missing-root degrades to `[]`
  rather than raising, no duplicates when patterns overlap). Uses
  `pathlib.Path.glob()` + `PurePath.full_match()` (Python 3.13+; this venv
  runs 3.14) for `**`-style gitignore-shape matching — deliberately the
  *same* `included_patterns`/`excluded_patterns` lists each
  `*-cocoindex-flows.py` already passes to
  `localfs.walk_dir(path_matcher=PatternFilePathMatcher(...))`, so pattern
  search's file set can't drift from what's actually indexed (vendor/,
  generated code, `node_modules/`, etc. excluded from both).
- **One new MCP tool per project**, added to the existing
  `cocoindex-code`/`koku-code`/`engram-code`/`dcm-code` `FastMCP` servers
  (no new server, per the "consolidate" ask) alongside each project's
  existing hybrid-search tool:
  - `cocoindex_pattern_search` (kubernaut) — 3 roots: kubernaut +
    kubernaut-operator (go), kubernaut-console (typescript/tsx, two
    languages in one root — handled by checking
    `detect_code_language(filename)` per file rather than tagging the root
    with one language). Takes `repo` to scope, same convention as the
    existing `cocoindex_search`.
  - `koku_code_pattern_search` (koku) — 1 root, python.
  - `engram_code_pattern_search` (engram) — 1 root, python (this repo's own
    source).
  - `dcm_code_pattern_search` (dcm) — 8 go roots (control-plane, cli,
    kubevirt/k8s-container/acm-cluster/three-tier/osac service providers,
    utilities); `shared-workflows` (shell/YAML) excluded — not a
    tree-sitter structural-pattern language. Takes `repo` to scope to one of
    the 8, mirroring `dcm_code_search`'s existing lack of per-repo
    filtering being a pre-existing gap I didn't otherwise touch.
  - Each tool wraps a new `pattern_search_code(pattern, language, limit,
    repo=None)` in its script: builds one `CodePattern` per call (reused
    across every candidate file, per the class's own docstring — "compiling
    a pattern is not free"), walks `chunking.find_code_files()`'s file list,
    calls `.match_file(path)` (which does its own cheap parse-free
    prefilter internally, so no separate `might_match()` call is needed),
    and renders matches via `render_match()` for enclosing-scope context.
    Stops early once `limit` matches are collected. Every function/CLI flag
    mirrors the existing `search_code`/`_format_results`/`_run_cli_query`
    naming so the two tools read as siblings, not a bolted-on afterthought.
  - Each script also gained a `--pattern PATTERN --language LANG[[ --repo
    REPO]` CLI mode for standalone debugging, parallel to the existing
    `--query`/`--mode` mode.
- **Smoke-tested against real live checkouts for all four** (not just
  synthetic fixtures, since `match_file()`/`render_match()` need a real
  tree-sitter parse to exercise meaningfully):
  - kubernaut: `func \NAME(\(A*\)) error` → 166/2927 go files matched;
    repo-scoped to `kubernaut-operator` → 2 results.
  - koku: `def \NAME(\(A*\)):` → 7191 matches across 1036 python files
    (unscoped smoke test); 2-result CLI run confirmed formatting.
  - engram: `def \NAME(\(A*\)):` against this repo's own live checkout
    (`ENGRAM_REPO_DIR` override, since the default points at the
    branch-scoped mirror) → correct matches (`backfill-*.py:main`, etc.).
  - dcm: `func \NAME(\(A*\)) error` → matches across `dcm-control-plane`;
    `--repo dcm-cli` scoping confirmed to return only `dcm-cli/...` paths.
  - No-match case confirmed to format as
    `No structural matches for language=X pattern: Y` rather than an
    empty/broken output.
- A leading module docstring containing a literal `\N` (as prose, inside a
  regular non-raw `"""..."""` string) broke all four scripts at import time
  with `SyntaxError: (unicode error) 'unicodeescape' codec can't decode
  bytes ... malformed \N character escape` the moment the `--pattern
  '...\NAME...'` usage example was added to the module docstring — Python
  interpreted `\N{` as the start of a named-character escape. Fixed by
  making each docstring a raw string (`r"""..."""`); this is a real gotcha
  specific to *documenting* `CodePattern`'s own `\`-sigil syntax in Python
  source, not a `CodePattern` issue itself.

**Docs updated**: `docs/COCOINDEX.md` gained a "Structural Pattern Search"
section (how it works, pattern-syntax cheat sheet, per-project tool/language
table, and an explicit "what this is NOT" subsection restating the
gopls/Serena non-replacement finding above so it's discoverable without
digging through this findings log). `README.md` gained one feature-bullet
line. Three of this repo's canonical Cursor rule templates that already
listed a "three-tier code guidance" section (`cursor/hindsight-memory.mdc`
— kubernaut/global, `cursor/koku-hindsight-memory.mdc`,
`cursor/operator-hindsight-memory.mdc`) each gained a fourth bullet for the
new pattern-search tool plus an explicit "not a substitute for
gopls/Serena" line, then `check-rule-sync.py --pair <name> --fix` was run
for those three specific pairs to propagate canonical → deployed.
**Deliberately left untouched**: `cursor/console-hindsight-memory.mdc` (no
three-tier section exists there — console is TypeScript with no LSP tool
configured, not applicable) and the `engram`/`console` rule pairs' *other*,
pre-existing drift that `check-rule-sync.py` (run with no `--pair` filter)
surfaced as a side effect of checking all pairs — both showed real,
unrelated drift (missing tag-scoping sections, a missing `max_tokens`
right-sizing section) that predates this session entirely and wasn't
introduced by this work; fixing it wasn't requested and risked silently
overwriting this very session's own currently-active operating rule
(`engram`'s deployed copy is *this repo's own* `.cursor/rules/hindsight-memory.mdc`).
Flagging it here rather than silently fixing or silently ignoring it.

**Also fixed in passing**: `tests/test_check_rule_sync.py`'s
`test_all_four_pairs_are_registered` was failing on an unrelated pre-existing
regression — the `koku` rule pair was added to `RULE_PAIRS` on 2026-08-03
without updating this pinning test's expected set, so it had been asserting
`{"global", "engram", "operator", "console"}` (4 pairs) against a dict that
actually has 5. Renamed to `test_all_five_pairs_are_registered` and fixed the
expected set. Caught because this same failure was called out as "1
pre-existing unrelated failure" in the previous entry's full-suite run, and
this session touches the exact file it lives next to.

**Full suite**: 462 passed, 0 failed (456 pre-existing + this session's 5 new
`TestFindCodeFiles` cases + the 1 `test_check_rule_sync.py` test that was
failing before this session's fix, now passing).
