## 2026-08-07 (same day, fourth follow-up): Capability Survey Triaged and Spiked — Batched Embeddings Shipped, `disposition_skepticism` Raised, `entity_labels`/`CodePattern` Spiked and Deliberately Not Implemented

**Trigger**: user asked to triage the previous entry's 8-item capability survey
into pros/cons/effort/risk per item (presented as a canvas), then: "let's
spike them all and investigate as required for further insight. Implement if
confidence is >90% the remaining ones."

**1. `SentenceTransformerEmbedder` batching — implemented (TDD, RED→GREEN),
confidence high, all four flow scripts done.** Added `chunking.embed_code_chunks()`
(wraps `cocoindex.ops.sentence_transformers.SentenceTransformerEmbedder`,
`asyncio.gather()`'d per-file so its `@coco.fn.as_async(batching=True)`
wrapper actually coalesces concurrent calls into one `model.encode()`) and
`chunking.code_embedding_dim()` (derives the pgvector column width from the
model instead of a hardcoded `vector(384)`). Replaced the ~15-line duplicated
`_embedder`/`_get_embedder()`/`_embed_text()` block and the sequential
per-chunk embed loop in `process_code_file()` across all four
`*-cocoindex-flows.py` scripts (kubernaut, `engram-`, `koku-`, `dcm-`) with
calls to the two new shared functions; each script's `code_main()` now reads
`embedding_dim = await chunking.code_embedding_dim()` before building its
`postgres.TableSchema`. New tests: `tests/test_chunking.py::TestEmbedCodeChunks`/
`TestCodeEmbeddingDim` (real model, real batching, asserts vector count/order/
dimension) plus `tests/test_engram_cocoindex_flows.py::TestProcessCodeFile`
(mocks `chunking.embed_code_chunks` for speed/determinism, asserts
`process_code_file()` declares one row per chunk with the right embedding by
position, including a 50-function synthetic file that forces a real multi-chunk
split). Full suite: 457 passed / 1 pre-existing unrelated failure
(`test_check_rule_sync.py`'s `koku` rule-pair check, untouched by this work).
**Not yet backfilled**: this changes `process_code_file`'s logic again, so
(per the previous entry's memoization finding) a `--mode backfill` on all
four projects would re-embed everything a second time today — deferred to a
separate session rather than bundled into this spike round, since the model
and its output vectors are unchanged, only the *call pattern* is, so there's
no correctness urgency.

**2. `cocoindex show`/`ls`/`update`/`drop` CLI — documented, no code change
needed.** Added a paragraph to `docs/COCOINDEX.md` (see this doc's git
history) demonstrating `cocoindex show --tree --recursive --target-states`
for answering "why hasn't file X reprocessed" without a manual SQL query;
zero-risk since it's read-only introspection already installed.

**3. `disposition_skepticism` on `cursor-memory` — implemented, 3→4.**
Verified current state via `get_bank` first (confirmed 3/3/3 exactly as the
survey assumed, no drift since). Raised only `disposition_skepticism` via
`update_bank(config_updates={"disposition_skepticism": 4})`, left
`literalism`/`empathy` untouched at 3 — this bank's job includes catching
contradictions (`contradiction_resolution.py`,
`docs/PENDING_CONTRADICTIONS.md`), so critical-evaluation level is the one
dimension directly on-target; the other two dispositions don't have an
equally clear directional case. Fully reversible via the same tool. **No
before/after measurement methodology was designed** — this is a
directionally-reasoned bet, not a validated improvement; if contradiction
catch-rate doesn't visibly improve over the following weeks, revert is a
one-call operation.

**4. `entity_labels` controlled vocabulary — spiked, deliberately NOT
implemented, confidence well under 90%.** Two independent findings, in
tension:
  - **Fragmentation confirmed real, but modest.** Pulled 500 `cursor-memory`
    memories (`list_memories`, 5×100 pages) and grouped their `entities`
    field by a case/punctuation-insensitive normalization. Of 577 distinct
    normalized entity names, exactly 2 had multiple raw-casing variants:
    `dcm-project` (12 occurrences) vs. `DCM project` (3) and
    `kubernautagent` (4) vs. `Kubernaut Agent` (4) — real fragmentation,
    confirming the previous entry's hypothesis, but a ~0.35% rate in this
    sample, not the widespread problem the original README-claim concern
    implied.
  - **Blocking uncertainty: unclear whether `entity_labels` would even fix
    this.** `update_bank`'s own schema describes it only as "Controlled
    vocabulary for entity classification" — ambiguous between (a) a fixed
    set of canonical *name* strings the extractor must normalize into (would
    directly fix the casing variants above) and (b) a fixed set of semantic
    *type/category* labels like the spaCy-style PERSON/ORG tagging (would
    not fix name-casing variants at all, only which categories get tagged).
    A `hindsight-docs` recall surfaced **directly contradictory** memory
    content on the more basic question of whether entities are even
    graph-traversable: one set of facts states "Engram uses zero-LLM entity
    extraction via spaCy ... The entity data is not used as a traversable
    graph"; another set states "Hindsight automatically builds a knowledge
    graph through entity extraction on every retain call ... tracking
    services, concepts, and patterns with co-occurrence edges" used by the
    `link_expansion` retriever. These likely describe two different code
    paths (an LLM-based extraction pipeline that does build a real
    co-occurrence graph, vs. a `fallback_extract.py` spaCy heuristic used
    only when the LLM API is down, per the 2026-08-03 finding) — but the
    memory bank itself doesn't disambiguate cleanly, and `hindsight-api`'s
    own source isn't available locally to check directly (it's a hosted
    service, not an installed package in `~/.hindsight/venv`). **Decision**:
    do not configure `entity_labels` this session. The confirmed fragmentation
    is real but small; the proposed fix's mechanism of action is unverified;
    guessing wrong risks either no effect (labels = categories) or
    over-constraining genuinely-new entity extraction (`entities_allow_free_form`
    would need to stay `true` regardless). Revisit only after getting a
    definitive answer on what `entity_labels` actually parameterizes, e.g. by
    asking Hindsight's maintainers/docs directly rather than inferring from
    an ambiguous one-line tool-schema description.

**Addendum (later same day, user follow-up): mechanism now confirmed via
Hindsight's own public docs (`hindsight.vectorize.io/developer/api/memory-banks`)
— reframes the fix, doesn't warrant a data wipe.** `entity_labels` is a
**`key:value` structured-classification system**, not free-form name
canonicalization: each label *group* declares a `key` (dimension name), a
`type` (`"value"` = pick one from a fixed enum, `"multi-values"`, `"text"`
= free-form, or `"map"` = structured sub-fields), and for enum types a
`values` allowlist that the retain LLM is schema-enforced to pick from
(`build_labels_model()` compiles it into a Pydantic model attached to the
structured-output call) — "anything outside the list is silently dropped."
Concretely, this means a bare "seed vocabulary of known good names" (the
original idea) isn't how this feature works at all: you don't fix
`"DCM project"` vs `"dcm-project"` by listing both as synonyms of one
canonical string. What *would* fix exactly that pair is defining a
`{"key": "project", "type": "value", "values": ["kubernaut", "koku", "dcm",
"engram"]}` label group — the LLM becomes structurally unable to emit
anything but one of those four exact strings for that dimension, and (with
`"tag": true`) it can simultaneously populate the memory's `tags` field.
The `kubernautagent`/`Kubernaut Agent` pair is a free-form *named entity*
(an agent/component name), not a classification dimension, and would need a
separate `component`/`agent` label group enumerating known service/component
names to get the same enforcement — real design work (an exhaustive,
maintained values list) beyond this session's scope, so only the `project`
dimension (exactly 4 values, zero ambiguity, already the exact set this repo
tags everything with) is proposed as a first cut. **This does not require
deleting any existing data**: it's a forward-only config change that shapes
future retains; the docs' entity/document export-import mechanism confirms
entities are keyed by canonical name and recomputed on any bank
re-import, with no "backfill re-labels old memories" path described, so a
wipe wouldn't even be the fast path to a fully-consistent label graph
regardless. Separately, `clear_memories` (the only deletion tool exposed)
takes an optional `fact_type` filter only, no tag/project filter — so
"delete kubernaut alone" isn't achievable with it as-is; the only available
granularity is the whole `cursor-memory` bank (shared across
kubernaut/koku/dcm/engram) or nothing. Given the confirmed fragmentation
rate is ~0.35% (2/577 sampled) and a data wipe wouldn't be scoped to
kubernaut anyway, **recommended path is: add the `project` label group going
forward, don't delete anything** — proposed to the user for confirmation
before applying, since it changes live retain behavior on a shared,
heavily-used bank.

**Applied**: user confirmed "add the `project` label group now," declined
the wider `component`/`agent` dimension (real design work, out of scope
today) and declined wiping any data. Configured via
`update_bank(config_updates={"entity_labels": [{"key": "project", "type":
"value", "optional": true, "tag": true, "values": [{"value": "kubernaut"},
{"value": "koku"}, {"value": "dcm"}, {"value": "engram"}]}]})` on
`cursor-memory`, leaving `entities_allow_free_form` at its default `true` so
regular named-entity extraction (the thing that's fragmenting) is
unaffected — this is additive, not a replacement. `tag: true` means every
future memory the LLM classifies also gets a `project:<value>` tag,
independent of and additional to whatever tag an ingestion script already
attaches. The MCP `get_bank` tool only echoes `bank_id`/`name`/
`disposition`/`mission` in its response (not the full resolved config), so
this couldn't be visually re-confirmed from the same tool that set it; the
`update_bank` call itself returned successfully with no error. Effect is
retain-time only — verifiable in practice the next time a
kubernaut/koku/dcm/engram-related fact is retained and checked for a
`project:*` entity/tag; not independently verified in this session.

**5. `recall`'s `min_scores`/`tag_groups` — `min_scores` confirmed
non-functional, not adopted; `tag_groups` left alone.** Tested `min_scores`
with the `"semantic"` key at multiple thresholds up to an extreme `0.99` —
result count and low-scoring items were essentially unchanged versus no
filter at all, indicating the parameter isn't being applied as documented
via this API surface. Not pursued further; documented here as a dead end so
it isn't re-attempted later without a reason to believe it now works.
`tag_groups` has no identified concrete use case given Engram's flat
single-list tagging scheme — left unused, not investigated further.

**6. `CodePattern`/`match_code`/`render_match` structural code search —
spiked directly against real files, confidence for production implementation
is NOT ≥90%, not implemented.**
  - **Python: works correctly.** The docstring's exact pattern
    (`def \NAME(\(A*\)):`) returns **zero** matches against any real,
    fully-type-annotated function (e.g. everything in `chunking.py`) —
    initially looked broken — because the pattern requires the arg-list's
    closing paren to be followed immediately by `:`, and every real function
    here has a `-> ReturnType` annotation in between. Adding an explicit
    `\RET` metavariable (`def \NAME(\(A*\)) -> \RET:`) fixed this
    immediately: matched all 8 functions in `chunking.py` correctly,
    capturing both name and return type
    (`split_code -> list[str]`, `code_embedding_dim -> int`, etc.).
    `render_match()` correctly rendered a clean signature-line view for a
    module-level function (no enclosing-scope frames needed, as expected).
  - **Go: broken for the realistic case (wildcard function bodies), as of
    cocoindex 1.0.18.** `func \NAME(\(A*\)) bool` alone (signature-only, no
    body) matches correctly. But appending *any* wildcard body form —
    `{\BODY*}`, `{\STMT*}`, even against a single-statement one-line body —
    consistently returns zero matches, while an exact **literal** body
    string does match. This means "find all Go functions matching signature
    X regardless of what's inside" — the actual query shape a
    `code_pattern_search` tool would need for kubernaut/DCM (both
    predominantly Go) — doesn't currently work with this API. Root cause
    not further isolated (no additional examples/tests ship in the
    installed package beyond the two module docstrings, and this is a
    closed-source PyO3/Rust extension with no local source to read) — could
    be a real upstream limitation specific to Go's tree-sitter grammar
    shape, or a pattern-syntax nuance not yet discovered.
  - **Decision**: not implemented. Python-only support would technically
    work, but the concrete motivating use case from the earlier Graphify
    comparison and this survey ("find all Go functions returning `(bool,
    error)`", `code_pattern_search` for kubernaut/DCM) is exactly the
    language where wildcard-body matching is currently broken. Revisit if a
    working Go wildcard-body pattern is found, or once demand is confirmed
    (per the original triage, nobody had asked for this yet).

**CORRECTION (later same day, user asked to check for an upstream issue
before filing one)**: **the "Go broken" finding above was wrong — it was
this session's own invalid pattern syntax, not a cocoindex bug.** Before
filing anything, searched `cocoindex-io/cocoindex`'s issues/PRs (`gh search
issues`) for prior art and found PR #2145, "`feat(code_match): containment
matcher `\{{ INNER \}}``" (merged 2026-06-20): the DSL's actual syntax for
"this block contains X" is the paired containment operator `\{{ ... \}}`
(double braces), not literal single Go braces `{ }` wrapping a made-up
`\BODY*`/`\STMT*` metavariable — which never existed as valid syntax in the
first place; every pattern tried in the original spike that combined literal
`{}` with a wildcard metavariable was syntactically invalid input, and the
matcher correctly returned zero matches for it rather than erroring, which
is what made it look like a silent bug instead of a mistake. Re-tested with
the real syntax and it works cleanly:
  - `func \NAME(\(A*\)) bool \{{ return \X \}}` (no literal braces around the
    containment operator) correctly matches `IsValid` and captures `X` as
    the returned expression.
  - More importantly, **the original motivating query needs no containment
    syntax at all**: `func \NAME(\(A*\)) (bool, error)` — a bare signature
    pattern with no body clause — matches "find all Go functions returning
    `(bool, error)`" directly and correctly, `kind='function_declaration'`,
    regardless of what's in the body. The DSL's "don't care about the rest"
    behavior is the *absence* of a body clause, not a wildcard placeholder
    inside one — there is no `\BODY*`-equivalent because none is needed for
    this query shape. `render_match()` confirmed working cleanly on this
    match too.
  - **No upstream issue filed** — there is no bug to report. The Python vs.
    Go asymmetry observed earlier (Python needing an explicit `-> \RET`
    metavariable, Go's bare-signature form working immediately) is a real
    but unsurprising grammar difference between the two languages' tree-sitter
    node shapes, not a defect in either case.
  - **Net effect on the CodePattern verdict**: the Go-language blocker is
    retracted, but the independent Serena/gopls-redundancy argument in the
    addendum below is untouched by this correction and still stands on its
    own — re-presented to the user rather than silently re-deciding, since
    removing one of two independent reasons for "skip" is exactly the kind
    of change that should go back to whoever made the call.

**Addendum (later same day, user follow-up): downgraded from "revisit later"
to "skip — redundant with already-adopted LSP tooling."** User pointed out
Engram already has comparable structural code capability in production:
Serena (onboarded 2026-07-31 as a gopls-equivalent for `koku`, Python/Django)
and `gopls` itself (already the standard Go analysis tool for
kubernaut/dcm/engram, per multiple recorded corrections this repo enforces —
"subagents must use available MCPs, cocoindex_search/gopls, not grep/rg").
Both are real language-server-backed tools (`find_symbol`,
`find_referencing_symbols`, diagnostics, outline) — a different mechanism
than `CodePattern`'s by-example AST pattern matching, but one that already
covers the practical day-to-day "find/navigate structurally" need this spike
was evaluating, with actual semantic precision (real type/reference
resolution) `CodePattern` can't offer. **Note**: this reasoning originally
also cited the "Go wildcard-body bug" as a second, compounding reason — that
part is retracted by the correction above (it was this session's own
invalid pattern syntax; Go actually works fine, including the exact
motivating "returns `(bool, error)`" query). The redundancy argument here is
independent of that correction and unaffected by it: `gopls`/Serena were
already in production before this spike and already answer the practical
symbol/reference-navigation need, which is a case for "skip" on its own
regardless of whether Go pattern matching works. **Final verdict: skip, not
implemented this session** — a narrower case than the original "revisit
later," but weaker than "never revisit" now that the Go blocker is gone;
worth reopening only if a concrete query shape emerges that neither `gopls`
nor Serena can answer (e.g. "find every place error-wrapping omits `%w`" —
a structural *shape* query with no fixed symbol name to anchor on, where
`CodePattern`'s by-example matching — now confirmed working for Go — would
add real value LSP tools can't).

**7. Directives — confirmed tag-gating, no new directives created (already
documented in the previous entry's survey; re-confirmed here as part of this
round's completion, no new investigation performed).**

**8. `reflect`'s `response_schema` — confirmed technically functional in a
prior test this session, not wired into `nightly-learn.py`; remains a design
decision for a future session, not implemented now.**

**Net result of this round**: 1 shipped (batched embeddings, full TDD,
tested), 1 config change applied (`disposition_skepticism`), 1 CLI usage
documented (zero code), 3 spiked and explicitly rejected for now
(`entity_labels`, `CodePattern`, `min_scores`) with the reasoning recorded
above so they aren't re-attempted blind, 2 previously-documented findings
carried forward without new work this round (directives, `response_schema`).
Nothing here met the user's ">90% confidence" bar for `entity_labels` or
`CodePattern` specifically because the spike itself surfaced a real blocking
unknown in each case (unclear label semantics; broken Go wildcard bodies) —
consistent with the whole point of spiking before committing.

---

## 2026-08-07 (same day, third follow-up): Backfilled the Tree-Sitter Chunking Fix on kubernaut/koku/engram — Found a Never-Deployed `koku` Code Index and a 24h+ Downed `kubernaut` Service Along the Way, Plus a CocoIndex/Hindsight Capability Survey

**Trigger**: user asked to back-fill the AST-aware chunking fix (previous
entry) onto "kubernaut's repos, koku and engram", and separately what other
CocoIndex/Hindsight capabilities Engram isn't using or has misconfigured,
similar to the tree-sitter case.

**Pre-flight state check turned up two unrelated, pre-existing incidents,
neither caused by today's change**:
- `io.vectorize.cocoindex.koku` was never loaded into launchd at all —
  `~/Library/LaunchAgents/` has no `io.vectorize.cocoindex.koku.plist`,
  even though the file exists checked-in under this repo's `launchd/`
  directory, and there was no `cocoindex.koku_code_embeddings` table in
  Postgres (`\dt cocoindex.*` listed only `code_embeddings`,
  `dcm_code_embeddings`, `engram_code_embeddings`,
  `correction_embeddings`) — koku's code index has never existed, despite
  koku being "onboarded" per the 2026-07-31 finding. Its docs/issues flows
  may be running some other way; only the code index's absence was
  confirmed here.
- `io.vectorize.cocoindex.service` (the original/kubernaut flow) is not
  currently loaded either — `launchctl list` returns "Could not find
  service", and `cocoindex-stderr.log`'s last line is a routine issues-poll
  completion at **2026-08-06 02:56**, over 24 hours before this session,
  with no crash/traceback around it (looks like a clean stop, not a
  crash). Kubernaut/operator/console docs, issues, and code have
  therefore not synced live since then. Left as-is and flagged here rather
  than unilaterally restarted — full live mode also resumes GitHub issue
  polling and doc watching, a broader change than "backfill the code
  index," and the clean-stop pattern (vs. a crash) leaves open that it was
  stopped deliberately for a reason not visible in this session.

**Backfill mechanics verified before running anything**: confirmed
`@coco.fn(memo=True, logic_tracking="full")` on `process_code_file` tracks
"own code + transitive children" as part of the memo fingerprint — so
changing what the function calls internally (yesterday's fix) invalidates
memoized results for every file even though file content didn't change; a
plain `--mode backfill` was not going to be a no-op. Confirmed empirically:
each of the three logs' first `[Stats]` block showed `1 reprocessed`
(kubernaut, engram) or `1 added` (koku, brand new table) against
`code_main`, not a skip.

**First backfill attempt was silently killed by the terminal session
tearing down**: launched via `nohup ... &` inside a single shell command:
the wrapper command itself returns immediately (correct, since the child
is backgrounded), but all three child processes vanished within
~90 seconds with no error, no traceback, and no completion line in their
log files — `nohup` protects against `SIGHUP` specifically, not a session
tool closing/reclaiming the whole process group. Fixed by issuing each
`python3 <flow>.py --apps code --mode backfill` command directly (not
wrapped in a manual `nohup ... &`) so the harness's own background-shell
tracking kept each process alive independently; all three then ran to
completion.

**Execution**: `engram-cocoindex-flows.py`'s live service (the only one of
the three actually running) was stopped via `launchctl unload` first, to
avoid a manual backfill and the live file-watcher writing to
`engram_code_embeddings` concurrently; kubernaut and koku had no live
service running to worry about. All three ran with `--apps code` (not the
default all-apps), scoping the backfill to just the code index and
avoiding an unnecessary full re-fetch of docs/issues/transcripts, which
this fix doesn't touch. Engram's live service was reloaded via `launchctl
load` immediately after its backfill finished.

**Results** (rows before → after; drops are expected and healthy — fewer,
more coherent chunks per file instead of extra fragments from a
mid-function cut):

| Project | Rows before | Rows after | Files | Time |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `kubernaut` | 19,270 | 17,992 | — | ~4m35s (part of combined run below) |
| `kubernaut-operator` | 920 | 842 | — | ↑ |
| `kubernaut-console` | 971 | 977 | — | ↑ |
| `engram` (self) | 1,383 | 1,235 | 74 | 21.7s |
| `koku` | 0 (table didn't exist) | 4,818 | 642 | 66.4s |

All three exit 0, zero errors in any log. Spot-checked a real
`kubernaut/test/services/mock-llm/scenarios/match_helpers.go` chunk and an
`engram/chunking.py` chunk post-backfill — both land on clean
function/module boundaries as expected from the previous entry's
synthetic test case.

---

### Capability survey: what else is available and unused/misconfigured

**CocoIndex (1.0.18, already installed — nothing here needs a new
dependency)**:

- **Zero embedding batching, confirmed from the backfill's own logs.**
  Every one of the ~25,000 chunks re-embedded above logged its own
  `Batches: 100%|██████████| 1/1` line — one `model.encode()` call per
  chunk, sequentially, exactly matching what `_embed_text()`'s
  single-string-at-a-time signature implies. CocoIndex ships
  `cocoindex.ops.sentence_transformers.SentenceTransformerEmbedder`, a
  built-in wrapper that (a) auto-batches concurrent `embed()` calls up to
  64 at a time via `@coco.fn.as_async(batching=True, max_batch_size=64)`,
  (b) auto-retries on accelerator OOM by halving the batch
  (`coco.RetryWithSmallerBatch`), (c) derives the pgvector column's
  dimension from the model itself (`__coco_vector_schema__`) instead of
  each of the four flow scripts hardcoding `vector(384)`, and (d) replaces
  ~15 duplicated lines of `_embedder`/`_get_embedder()`/`_embed_text()`
  boilerplate in each of the four `*-cocoindex-flows.py` scripts with one
  shared instance. Realizing the batching benefit needs one more change
  alongside swapping the embedder in: `process_code_file`'s loop currently
  `await`s each chunk's embedding one at a time, so calls are never
  actually concurrent; issuing them via e.g. `asyncio.gather()` per file
  (or across the whole file batch) is what lets the batcher actually
  coalesce multiple chunks into one `model.encode()` call. Not done as
  part of this session — a real, separate, low-risk perf/dedup follow-up.
- **`cocoindex.ops.code.CodePattern`/`match_code`/`index_terms` — a
  built-in structural code-matching engine, unused.** This directly
  revisits the "could CocoIndex support Graphify-style structural
  queries?" question from earlier today: CocoIndex already ships
  by-example AST pattern matching with metavariables (e.g.
  `CodePattern(r"def \NAME(\(ARGS*\)):", language="python")`,
  `cp.match_source(...)` → matched node + captured metavariable spans),
  a cheap parse-free `might_match()` prefilter, and `index_terms()`
  (extracts identifiers + string-literal content for building an external
  FTS/prefilter index) — plus `render_match()`, which renders a match with
  its enclosing function/class signature as context frames, elided
  correctly, ready to hand back from a search tool instead of a raw
  chunk. None of this is wired into `code_app` today. This doesn't
  replicate Graphify's NetworkX/Leiden call-graph clustering, but the
  primitive Graphify itself builds on (structural AST matching) is
  already sitting in the installed dependency, unused — a
  `code_pattern_search`-style MCP tool ("find all Go functions returning
  `(bool, error)`", "find all React components calling `useState`")
  looks buildable with zero new dependencies.
- **`cocoindex show`/`ls`/`update`/`drop` CLI, unused.** The installed
  CLI (`cocoindex.cli`) already exposes flow/target-state introspection
  (`show --tree`, `--recursive`, `--target-states`) that could replace
  some custom debugging (e.g. answering "why hasn't file X been
  reprocessed" without a manual SQL query) — noted in passing in the
  2026-07-xx version-bump entry as a changelog line item, never actually
  tried.
- **Not a gap, checked and ruled out**: the postgres connector
  (`cocoindex.connectors.postgres`) has no built-in tsvector/BM25 helper —
  `code_app`'s hand-rolled `declare_sql_command_attachment` trigger SQL for
  `search_vector` is the correct, necessary approach, not a missed
  built-in.

**Hindsight** (checked via `get_bank`/`list_directives` against the two
banks reachable in this session, `cursor-memory` and `engram-docs` —
findings may not generalize to `kubernaut-docs`/`kubernaut-issues`, not
independently checked here):

- **Bank disposition is at the untouched default (3/3/3) on both banks
  checked.** `skepticism`/`literalism`/`empathy` are each configurable
  1–5 via `update_bank`; both `cursor-memory` and `engram-docs` are
  running the same default 3/3/3 as every other bank. This is the most
  directly on-target lever found: `cursor-memory`'s whole job includes
  catching contradictions (`contradiction_resolution.py`,
  `docs/PENDING_CONTRADICTIONS.md`, the correction-enforcement hooks) —
  raising its `disposition_skepticism` is a plausible, cheap experiment
  for that exact goal, never tried. Both banks' `mission` fields
  (`reflect_mission`/`retain_mission`) are also empty strings — no
  bank-specific steering beyond Hindsight's own defaults.
- **Directives exist but are used almost by accident, not systematically.**
  Found exactly one (`cursor-memory`: "HAPI deprecated - historical only",
  priority 10, created 2026-07-01) via ad hoc MCP tool use in a past
  chat session — grepping the whole Engram codebase for
  `create_directive`/`directive` turns up zero hits, confirming no
  ingestion script or pipeline creates or manages them; `engram-docs` has
  none at all. Directives are the one native Hindsight mechanism for
  standing cross-session guidance ("always treat X as historical",
  "prefer Y source over Z when they conflict") — right now that job is
  done entirely by Cursor-side `.mdc` rule files instead, which Hindsight
  itself has no visibility into during `reflect()`.
- **`entity_labels` (controlled vocabulary for entity classification) is
  unset — entities are freeform.** Given the README's "knowledge graph —
  entities link across sessions" is a headline feature, and linking only
  works when the same real-world thing gets the same entity name twice,
  freeform LLM-assigned names for the same repeated entities (e.g.
  "DataStorage" vs "datastorage" vs "DS") plausibly fragments the
  co-occurrence graph into disconnected near-duplicate nodes instead of
  one well-linked node — not confirmed by inspecting the actual graph
  here, but a controlled vocabulary seeded with known recurring names
  (service/repo/component names across kubernaut/koku/dcm/engram) is a
  concrete, cheap thing to try and measure.
- **`recall`'s `min_scores` and `tag_groups` params are unused** (grepped
  zero hits) — `min_scores` could suppress the long tail of
  weakly-relevant results a broad recall pulls in (e.g. several 0.70-ish
  semantic-score facts mixed into a mandatory methodology recall this
  session); `tag_groups` allows boolean tag combinations beyond flat
  `tags`/`tags_match`, not obviously needed given Engram's current
  single-flat-list tagging scheme.
- **Not investigated further, flagged only**: `reflect`'s `response_schema`
  (structured JSON output — `nightly-learn.py`'s `reflect()`/`reflect_windowed()`
  calls consume free text today) and `mcp_enabled_tools` (per-bank tool
  allowlisting).

**Not done**: no code changes from the capability survey — these are
findings to evaluate and prioritize, not fixes; unlike the tree-sitter gap,
none of these are "docs claim X, code doesn't do X" bugs, they're genuine
unused-but-available levers.

---

## 2026-08-07 (same day, second follow-up): `code_app` Docs Claimed Tree-Sitter Chunking Since Inception — It Never Actually Called It, Fixed in All Four `*-cocoindex-flows.py` Scripts

**Trigger**: while assessing whether CocoIndex could be extended to support
Graphify-style structural-graph queries (see the Graphify entry above), the
user asked to clarify an apparent contradiction: earlier in the same
conversation it was stated that "cocoindex" lacked tree-sitter support, then
later that the gap was in how Engram configures CocoIndex — these sounded
mutually exclusive.

**Root cause, once separated into layers**: the upstream `cocoindex` package
(1.0.18, already installed in `~/.hindsight/venv`) has mature, first-class
tree-sitter support — `cocoindex.ops.text.detect_code_language()` (extension
→ language name) and `RecursiveSplitter` (AST-aware chunking that descends
function/type/block nodes, falling back to a raw character cut only for a
single node too large to fit `chunk_size` on its own) are both public,
documented exports (`__all__` in that module). Verified empirically: a
120-char `chunk_size` against a two-function Go sample split cleanly into one
chunk per function, never mid-declaration. **Engram's own flow code never
called either of them.** All four `*-cocoindex-flows.py` scripts
(`cocoindex-flows.py` for kubernaut, plus `engram-`, `koku-`,
`dcm-cocoindex-flows.py`) declared an identical `process_code_file()` that
called `_split_text()` — a thin alias straight to `chunking.split_fixed_window()`,
the plain character-offset slicer — with no language awareness at all. This
was a pure docs-vs-implementation drift, not a missing upstream capability:
`README.md`, `docs/COCOINDEX.md`, and `docs/README.md` had all described the
code index as tree-sitter/AST-parsed since the flow was first written, and
`docs/INSTALL.md` even told new installers to `pip install tree-sitter
tree-sitter-go` as an "optional, for custom symbol extraction" step —
misleading on two counts: nothing in the codebase imported either package,
and cocoindex's own bundled tree-sitter support needs no separate install at
all (confirmed: `pip show tree-sitter` finds nothing in the venv, yet
`RecursiveSplitter` parses Go/Python/Bash/YAML/TypeScript/TSX correctly
regardless).

**Fix (TDD — RED then GREEN)**: added `chunking.split_code(text, filename,
chunk_size, chunk_overlap)` to the shared `chunking.py` (symlinked into
`~/.hindsight/`, imported by all four flow scripts already), backed by a
module-level `RecursiveSplitter()` singleton and
`detect_code_language(filename=...)`. Six new tests in
`tests/test_chunking.py::TestSplitCode` were written first and confirmed
failing (`AttributeError: module 'chunking' has no attribute 'split_code'`)
before the function existed, covering: single-chunk short files, the
plain-`list[str]` return contract (must match `split_fixed_window()`
exactly, since `code_app` has no stable-key/delta-retain concept — see the
2026-08-03 chunking rewrite), the core regression (a `chunk_size` that fits
each function individually but not the whole file must produce one chunk per
function, never a signature without its closing brace), language detection
by extension (Python vs. Go), graceful degradation for an unrecognized
extension (no language grammar ≠ an error — `RecursiveSplitter` still does
generic paragraph/line-aware splitting), and empty input. All four flow
scripts' `process_code_file()` now call
`chunking.split_code(content, filename=filepath, chunk_size=1000,
chunk_overlap=300)` instead of `_split_text(...)`; the now-dead `_split_text`
alias (and its one remaining unit test, in
`tests/test_engram_cocoindex_flows.py`, which tested the alias directly) was
deleted from all four files rather than left as unreachable code — its only
caller in each file was `process_code_file`. Full suite: 449 passed (plus one
pre-existing, unrelated `test_check_rule_sync.py` failure about a stale
`koku` pair expectation, confirmed present before this change too via `git
stash`).

**Docs corrected to match reality** (not just to match the new
implementation — the previous wording overclaimed even relative to what
`RecursiveSplitter` actually does): `docs/COCOINDEX.md`'s code-flow
paragraph no longer claims chunks are keyed by "file path, line range, and
package name as metadata" (the `CodeEmbedding` row only ever had `filepath`
+ positional `chunk_index`); `README.md`'s hybrid-search bullet no longer
says tree-sitter "extracts functions, types, and methods" (it chunks
*around* those AST boundaries, it doesn't extract/name them as discrete
symbols); `docs/INSTALL.md` no longer tells installers to
`pip install tree-sitter tree-sitter-go` — replaced with a note that
`cocoindex` bundles this already.

**Not done, deliberately deferred**: no backfill/truncate of the
`code_embeddings` / `engram_code_embeddings` / `koku_code_embeddings` /
`dcm_code_embeddings` pgvector tables was run as part of this fix. Existing
rows keep their old fixed-window chunk boundaries and IDs (`{filepath}:{i}`)
until the next full walk reprocesses each file — memoized `@coco.fn(memo=True)`
means unchanged files won't be reprocessed until touched or a backfill is
explicitly triggered (`python3 <flow>.py --mode backfill`). This is a
correctness improvement for chunk *boundaries*, not a schema change, so
stale rows are stale in *content* only (a function that would no longer be
split by AST-aware chunking may still show up pre-split in old rows), not
broken; running a full backfill on all four tables is a follow-up, not
folded into this fix.

---

## 2026-08-07 (same day, follow-up): Comparative Analysis Against supermemoryai/supermemory — Closed-Source Core Blocks Component-Level Comparison, One Issue Filed for MemoryBench's Harness Pattern

**Trigger**: user asked how Engram/its individual components compare against
[supermemoryai/supermemory](https://github.com/supermemoryai/supermemory),
prompted by seeing it referenced "in the benchmarks" (MemoryBench, see
below).

**Method**: shallow-cloned `supermemoryai/supermemory` (229 MB monorepo) and
inventoried `apps/` (browser-extension, docs, mcp, memory-graph-playground,
raycast-extension, web) and `packages/` (memory-graph, ai-sdk, tools,
validation, various framework SDKs). Read `apps/mcp/src/server/*`,
`packages/memory-graph/src/*`, and `packages/validation/schemas.ts`, plus the
live README and `supermemoryai/memorybench`'s README.

**Key structural difference from the mem0 comparison — the core engine is
closed source**: unlike mem0 (`mem0/memory/main.py`, `scoring.py`,
`entity_extraction.py` all readable), this public monorepo contains no
extraction, contradiction-resolution, or forgetting logic at all. What's
open here is the web dashboard/consumer app, browser extension, a thin MCP
protocol layer (`apps/mcp/src/server/` — container-tags/spaces/widgets, no
extraction), SDK wrappers, and a graph **visualization** component
(`packages/memory-graph` — canvas rendering only, confirmed by grepping for
`contradiction`/`forgett`/`expir` and finding zero matches outside
`packages/validation/schemas.ts`'s API-level field names `expiresAt`/
`isStatic`). The actual Memory Engine backing `api.supermemory.ai` and the
self-hosted `supermemory-server` binary isn't published. This makes a
source-level comparison impossible; claims about contradiction-handling or
forgetting quality are unverifiable from outside, unlike the mem0 review.

**Component comparison, mostly corroborates the mem0 finding rather than
surfacing something new**: same "different problem shape" verdict as mem0
(2026-07-31 entry above), reinforced by an even heavier product footprint
(consumer app, browser extension, billing/OAuth, connectors) and the
closed-source core. Specific points: (1) **forgetting/TTL**
(`expiresAt`) — same "poor domain fit" reasoning as mem0's TTL finding,
not a new gap, Engram's content isn't calendar-bound; (2) **profile
freshness** (claimed ~50ms static/dynamic profile) — a smaller gap than it
first appears, since `cursor-memory`'s 3 mental models already refresh
after every consolidation (only `kubernaut-docs`/`kubernaut-issues` models
lag on manual/nightly refresh), and there's no visibility into how
Supermemory affords real-time synthesis affordably, so adopting it would be
speculative rather than evidenced (same reasoning as the #6 closure);
(3) **multi-modal extraction** (OCR, video transcription, AST-aware code
chunking) — same conclusion as the Graphify entry above, no measured
Engram need, and here also unverifiable/non-self-hostable since it's
closed; (4) **connectors** (Drive/Gmail/Notion/GitHub, real-time webhooks)
— GitHub overlap already covered differently (5-min poll vs. webhook, no
measured need for sub-5-min issue freshness), others have no Engram use
case; (5) **hybrid search** (single fused `searchMode: hybrid` call) —
different implementation shape for an outcome Engram already gets via
parallel multi-bank MCP recall, not a capability gap.

**The one actionable finding: MemoryBench's harness design, not its
scores**. [MemoryBench](https://github.com/supermemoryai/memorybench) is
Supermemory's own open-source (MIT), pluggable benchmark harness comparing
memory providers (supermemory, mem0, zep) on LoCoMo/LongMemEval/ConvoMem,
with a checkpointed pipeline (ingest→index→search→answer→evaluate→report,
resumable per stage), judge-agnostic evaluation (swap GPT-4o/Claude/Gemini
without code changes), and a deliberately-uncollapsed triple `MemScore`
(accuracy/latency/tokens) "because collapsing quality, latency, and cost
into one score hides important tradeoffs." The #8 domain-mismatch reasoning
still fully applies to the raw LoCoMo/LongMemEval/ConvoMem *numbers* (still
persona/conversational-memory tasks, still nothing like this repo's actual
correction/convention-extraction input) — but the harness *architecture*
itself is a reusable pattern that #10's current 7-case pilot script doesn't
have (no checkpoint/resume, single hardcoded exact-match scorer, no
latency/token tracking).

**Outcome**: **one lightweight enhancement issue filed**,
[#12](https://github.com/jordigilh/engram/issues/12) — hardens #10's
benchmark harness with three specific MemoryBench-inspired patterns
(checkpointing, pluggable/judge-agnostic scoring, structured triple
hit-rate/latency/tokens report), explicitly *not* an adoption of
MemoryBench, Supermemory, or any new dependency — three design patterns
re-implemented locally, sequenced after #10's base implementation. No other
issues filed: the remaining overlap surface is either already covered
differently (hybrid search, GitHub connector), a poor domain fit
(TTL/forgetting, same as mem0), or unverifiable/inapplicable given the
closed-source core (contradiction resolution, multi-modal extraction,
profile-freshness mechanism).

## 2026-08-07: Comparative Analysis Against Graphify-Labs/graphify — Real Gap Identified, Deferred on Maturity and Resource-Cost Grounds

**Trigger**: user asked for an evaluation of
[Graphify-Labs/graphify](https://github.com/Graphify-Labs/graphify) for engram
integration, pros and cons.

**Method**: fetched repo metadata via the GitHub API (stars, forks,
contributors, commit history, releases, open/closed issue counts), read the
live README, `ARCHITECTURE.md`, `pyproject.toml`, and `SECURITY.md` from the
repo, and compared its design against this repo's own `docs/COCOINDEX.md` and
`docs/README.md` (Hindsight's entity graph vs. CocoIndex's hybrid code
search).

**What it is**: a Claude Code/Cursor/Codex/Gemini-CLI agent skill that builds
a deterministic knowledge graph — tree-sitter AST + a call-graph pass for
code, agent-LLM-driven concept extraction for docs/PDFs/images — using
NetworkX + Leiden community detection, exportable to
`graph.json`/HTML/Obsidian/wiki, and servable as a local MCP stdio server
directly off a saved graph file.

**Real gap identified, not pure overlap**: unlike this repo's earlier
comparisons against mem0/tstockham96-engram (both memory SDKs, see the
2026-07-29 and 2026-07-31 entries above), graphify solves a different
question class than anything currently in this stack. This repo's
`code-index` (dense+BM25/RRF via `cocoindex_search`) answers
similarity/exact-identifier queries; Hindsight's entity graph does
single-hop co-occurrence expansion over memory facts (`docs/README.md`'s
3-tier recall hierarchy). Neither answers structural questions — call
graphs, shortest path between two functions, blast-radius — which
graphify's tree-sitter call-graph pass is purpose-built for, and its
`--mcp` mode would drop in as a fourth MCP server with no custom glue code.

**Deferred, not rejected — two independent concerns raised by the user**:
(1) **maturity** — pre-1.0 (v0.9.35), with near-daily releases where nearly
every commit is a per-language extraction bugfix (Kotlin, C#, Ruby, Java,
callflow direction), a `pyproject.toml` MIT vs. GitHub-detected Apache-2.0
license mismatch, and a stars (103,799) / forks (10,076) / contributor-
concentration profile (#1 contributor ~950 commits vs. #2 at 27, org only 5
weeks old) unusual enough on a 4-month-old repo to warrant independent
verification before treating popularity as a trust signal; (2) **resource
cost** — this repo already runs CocoIndex + Hindsight + embedded Postgres as
always-on launchd services, and graphify's dependency stack (`networkx`,
`graspologic`/Leiden, a dozen `tree-sitter-*` grammars) would add meaningful
weight on top of that for a capability that's genuinely useful but not
currently blocking any active work.

**Outcome**: **not integrated.** User's explicit call: the gain doesn't
clearly outweigh the maturity risk and added resource footprint on an
already-running CocoIndex/Hindsight/Postgres stack. No GitHub issue filed —
this is a "revisit later" deferral, not a confirmed need, and filing now
would repeat the exact mistake #6/#7's closures corrected for (proposing
against a hypothetical rather than a measured one). Recorded here so the
comparison doesn't need to be redone from scratch; revisit if graphify
reaches a stable 1.0 (slower fix cadence across language extractors) or if a
concrete internal need for call-graph/structural queries surfaces.

## 2026-08-06: Hook-Delivered PR Review Checklist — Extending the Correction-Enforcement Hooks to Inject Reviewer Expectations, Not Just Block Contradictions

**Context**: `osac-service-provider`'s `CLAUDE.md` grew a "Pre-PR review
checklist" section distilling recurring reviewer feedback across several
PRs — error mapping (`grpcstatus.Errorf` vs. bare `fmt.Errorf`), response
field consistency across return paths, verify-don't-assume for external
data shapes, comment/decision length, and `DD-*`-vs-exploration placement.
The ask: surface this automatically during implementation planning so it
stops being something a human reviewer has to repeat. Two options were
explicitly ruled out up front:

- **Editing `CLAUDE.md` directly** — this file is read by every
  contributor's Claude Code, most of whom don't use Engram/Hindsight at
  all. Injecting Engram-specific process content there is conflictive with
  a shared, non-Engram project file.
- **Relying on `AGENTS.md`/rule-file content in general** — "it's been
  proven enough times that the model will ignore at will the contents of
  CLAUDE.md and AGENTS.md" (direct user framing). Advisory context that a
  model can choose to skip, or that a context-summarization pass can
  silently drop, isn't a fix for a *recurring* problem.

**Design: reuse the existing hook pair, add a third member sharing its
marker.** The 2026-08-03 Deterministic Correction Enforcement hooks
(`detect-plan-kickoff.sh` + `post-plan-hindsight-check.py`, see below)
already had exactly the right shape: a `beforeSubmitPrompt` detector that
fires once per plan kickoff, handing off to tool-call hooks via a
per-session marker file. Adding `post-plan-checklist-reminder.py`
(`postToolUse`, same matcher, same marker) as a third member was cheaper
and more consistent than inventing a parallel mechanism.

**Spike before implementing**: the one genuinely unverified assumption —
that `postToolUse` fires with the *same* `session_id` as `preToolUse` for
the same tool call (only checked for `preToolUse`↔`beforeSubmitPrompt`
previously) — was spiked with a disposable logging hook pair before
writing any real code. Confirmed: identical `session_id` across
`preToolUse` and `postToolUse` for one `Shell` call. This is what makes the
hand-off safe: the reminder hook can look up the same marker the enforcer
already read.

**Blast radius was the hard part, not the feature itself.**
`post-plan-hindsight-check.py` is one canonical file, symlinked identically
into all 17 previously-onboarded repos (3 kubernaut + 14 dcm-project). The
checklist feature needed the enforcer's marker-deletion timing to change
(defer deletion so the reminder hook can still read it after an allow) —
but only for the one repo that actually has a checklist
(`osac-service-provider`), not for the other 16. Two framings were
considered:

- Gate on a coarse `project == "dcm"` label — rejected: it would make *all*
  14 dcm-project repos' behavior technically different even though 13 of
  them have no checklist to defer for, and it hardcodes a project taxonomy
  into a script that shouldn't need to know it.
- **Gate on file existence** (chosen): the enforcer checks whether
  `~/.hindsight/review-checklists/<repo>.md` exists for the marker's `repo`
  field before deciding whether to defer deletion at all. No file → exactly
  today's unconditional-immediate-unlink behavior, byte-for-byte. This
  makes the behavioral change provably scoped to precisely the repos with a
  checklist file (one, today), not to a label — adding a checklist for
  another repo later is "add a file + commit," no script change, and
  kubernaut can never be affected since it will never have a matching file.

Both the shared enforcer's tests and the new reminder's tests explicitly
cover the "no checklist file for this repo" path (not just the
has-checklist path) specifically to make this claim verifiable, not just
asserted.

**Content storage tradeoff**: a bare file under `~/.hindsight/` has no
tamper-visibility (same trust boundary as the hook scripts themselves — a
same-user local attacker could modify either one identically). Storing the
checklist as a git-tracked file inside *engram's own repo*
(`hooks/review-checklists/osac-service-provider.md`) and symlinking the
whole directory into `~/.hindsight/review-checklists/` (mirroring the
existing pattern for `contradiction_resolution.py`) gives a real integrity
signal — tampering shows up in `git diff`/`git log` — without ever touching
`osac-service-provider`'s own repo. A lightweight `is_safe_checklist_content()`
runtime check (max ~3000 chars, no URLs, no obvious
prompt-injection phrasing like "ignore previous instructions") was kept as
cheap defense-in-depth on top, explicitly scoped as *not* a substitute for
the git-history control — it also catches accidental corruption, not just
malicious edits.

**Gap discovered and closed while scoping this**: `kubernaut-v1.5` and
`kubernaut-v1.6` (two additional local kubernaut worktree clones) turned
out to have *no* `.cursor/hooks.json` at all — the original
`kubernaut_dcm`-scoped rollout of the base hook pair on 2026-08-03 missed
them entirely. `hooks/install.sh` was extended with kubernaut-path
auto-detection (any path containing `/kubernaut`) specifically so this
backfill — and any future kubernaut clone — gets exactly the base
detector+enforcer pair, never the checklist reminder, without a separate
code path.

**Outcome**: `hooks/_write_plan_marker.py` gained `detect_repo_name()` and
a `repo` marker field; `post-plan-hindsight-check.py`'s marker-deletion
became conditional on checklist-file existence (with a `deny` outcome
still deleting immediately regardless, since `postToolUse` never fires
after a `preToolUse` deny — confirmed in the original 2026-08-03 spike);
`hooks/install.sh` and `_merge_hooks_json.py` gained a 4th optional CLI arg
and kubernaut auto-detection; all 14 dcm-project repos were re-installed to
pick up the third hook, `kubernaut`/`kubernaut-console`/`kubernaut-operator`
were left untouched entirely, and `kubernaut-v1.5`/`kubernaut-v1.6` were
backfilled with the base pair only. New/updated tests cover repo detection,
the conditional-unlink matrix (no-file / allow-with-file / deny-with-file /
timeout-with-file), the 3-hook merge (including the kubernaut
empty-reminder-arg case), and the reminder hook's sanity-check and
marker-cleanup behavior.

## 2026-08-05: Two Postgres Outages in Two Days — pg0-Embedded's Self-Managed Lifecycle Was the Actual Root Cause, Fixed by Decoupling It

**Context**: `hindsight` and `cocoindex-code` MCP tools both failed with
"connection refused on `localhost:5432`" this morning. This is the *second*
Postgres outage in as many days sharing the same underlying cause class:
`hindsight-api`'s embedded `pg0` Postgres owns its own start/stop lifecycle,
and two independent things can take that lifecycle down without touching
Postgres's actual data at all.

**Incident 1 (2026-08-04)**: a stale PID bug. `pg0`'s liveness check reads
the PID out of `<data_dir>/postmaster.pid` and only verifies *some* process
holds that PID number — not that it's actually `postgres` (no
`/proc/<pid>/comm`/cmdline check). After a reboot, a recycled low PID got
reassigned to an unrelated process (`TextInputMenuAgent`), so
`ensure_running()` false-positived and skipped starting Postgres entirely,
producing a silent crash loop. Fixed that day by invalidating the stale PID
in `instance.json` and re-running `pg0 start`. This is an upstream bug in the
`pg0-embedded` package itself, not something patchable in Engram code.

**Incident 2 (2026-08-05, ~01:00)**: a different mechanism, same symptom.
The nightly `hindsight-blue-green-restart.sh` job (see
[FINDINGS.md](../FINDINGS.md) 2026-08-02 for why it exists) failed its
`launchctl bootstrap` call with "Bootstrap failed: 5: Input/output error" —
consistent with `gui/<uid>` bootstrap requiring an active Aqua (GUI login)
session, which wasn't available at 1 AM. Around the same time, the embedded
Postgres received a clean `pg_ctl stop -m fast`. `hindsight-api` itself
relaunched via its own `KeepAlive`, but nothing relaunched Postgres — the app
came back up in a degraded state, unable to connect to its database.

**Initial misdiagnosis**: before the actual cause was found, the
investigation first assumed a Podman-managed Postgres (wrong — this project
doesn't use Podman for Postgres) and then a Homebrew `postgresql@16` (also
wrong, and installing it created a *second*, unrelated problem: a real port
conflict with `pg0` on 5432 once `pg0` tried to restart). Both dead ends were
directly corrected by the user before the actual `pg0`-embedded architecture
was identified. Notably, hindsight-docs already had this exact lesson
recorded from a prior incident — *"connection refused on port 5432 doesn't
necessarily indicate the expected Postgres instance died; requires checking
which Postgres is expected first (`DEFAULT_DATABASE_URL`, `pg0`, or
embedded-db config) before consulting system package manager"* — but it
wasn't recalled at the start of this investigation, since recalling it
requires `hindsight` to be up, which was itself the thing that was down. A
documented lesson that depends on the very system it's warning about being
broken has an inherent blind spot; the fix below removes the underlying
fragility instead of relying on remembering the lesson faster next time.

**Fix — decouple Postgres from both failure surfaces.** Both incidents share
one root cause: Postgres's uptime was coupled to `hindsight-api`'s own
process lifecycle and to a restart script's ability to successfully
`launchctl bootstrap`. Neither has anything to do with Postgres itself.
Postgres now runs as its own independently-supervised launchd LaunchAgent
(`launchd/io.vectorize.hindsight.postgres.plist`), using the exact same PG
18.1.0 binary and the same 12GB data directory (`~/.pg0/instances/hindsight/data`,
extensions `vector 0.8.1` + `pg_trgm 1.6` already installed) — no data
migration needed. `hindsight-api` connects to it via a plain
`HINDSIGHT_API_DATABASE_URL=postgresql://hindsight:hindsight@localhost:5432/hindsight`
in `config.env`, which makes `parse_pg0_url()` return `is_pg0=False` and
bypasses `EmbeddedPostgres`/`resolve_database_url()` entirely — confirmed via
`engine/memory_engine.py` and `engine/db/postgresql.py`; note the URL must be
a plain libpq-style string, not `postgresql+asyncpg://`, which crashes
`asyncpg.create_pool()` on the hot path. `COCOINDEX_PG_URL` already pointed
at this same instance/database and needed no change — CocoIndex and
Hindsight were already sharing one physical Postgres, just one that could be
brought down as a side effect of two things unrelated to it.

One deliberate behavior change made at the same time: `fsync` was switched
from `off` (pg0's default, replicated via undocumented `-c` flags baked into
its Rust binary — confirmed via `strings`, not read from any config file) to
`on`, since this is now permanent always-on infrastructure rather than a
disposable embedded instance, and the durability trade-off is no longer
implicit.

**Verified**: cutover took under a minute (stop old instance, bootstrap new
plist, `pg_isready`, confirm `SHOW fsync` is `on`); both `hindsight` recall
and `engram_code_search` MCP tools worked immediately after; killing the new
postmaster's PID directly (`kill -9`) confirmed launchd's `KeepAlive`
relaunches it within ~10s (throttled by `ThrottleInterval: 5`) with zero
script or manual intervention — the exact property neither prior incident
had. As a side effect of restarting both `hindsight-api` colors to pick up
the new `DATABASE_URL`, also discovered and fixed that the "blue" instance
had been silently unloaded from launchd entirely (likely the same
bootstrap-without-Aqua-session failure class, on a previous restart cycle)
and re-bootstrapped it.

**Follow-up not done**: the restart script's `launchctl bootstrap` failure
under a non-Aqua session is still unresolved as a *general* launchd
limitation — it just no longer matters for Postgres specifically, since
nothing ever calls `bootstrap` on its job after the one-time initial
install.

---

## 2026-08-04 (same day, follow-up): Deterministic Correction Enforcement — Advisory Rules Can't Be the Whole Answer, So We Built a Harness Hook That Can't Be Skipped

**Context**: recurring concern that the same corrections (TDD RED/GREEN/REFACTOR
phases collapsed into one, methodology gaps) keep happening day after day
despite the nightly learning pipeline and cursor rules already documenting
them. Direct question: is this a tooling/harness problem rather than a
memory-content problem? Diagnosis landed on a **compliance-verification
gap**: `recall()` and rule files are advisory — a model can choose not to
call `recall`, and a summarized/compacted context can silently drop a rule's
instructions, so a *documented* convention and an *enforced* one are not the
same thing. Cursor hooks were identified as the one mechanism the model
cannot simply decide to skip, matching the explicit ask: "harness hooks is
not an option for the model to ignore" (unlike `AGENTS.md`/rule files, which
are).

**Design goal**: a hook pair that, on plan-implementation kickoff, checks
the plan's stated approach against `cursor-memory` for a real semantic
contradiction with an established convention, and — only on a genuine
conflict — hard-blocks the first real tool call with an explanation, rather
than a soft/ignorable warning.

**Spike 1 — `CreatePlan` itself is unhookable.** A `postToolUse` hook
matched on `CreatePlan` never fired, with or without an explicit `matcher`.
Ruled out entirely as an attach point.

**Spike 2 — `beforeSubmitPrompt` fires reliably but can't inject context.**
Cursor auto-submits a fixed boilerplate message
("Implement the plan as specified... Don't stop until you have completed
all the to-dos.") immediately after a plan is confirmed through the UI.
`beforeSubmitPrompt` fires reliably on this and matches the same
`session_id` a later `preToolUse` call on the same turn sees — a usable
correlation key. But two things it does *not* give: (1) despite docs
suggesting `attachments` carries the confirmed plan, that field was
consistently empty in every real capture — worked around with a
"newest-`mtime`-under-`~/.cursor/plans/`" heuristic instead; (2) it cannot
inject `additional_context`/`user_message` into the model's turn at all —
confirmed by direct empirical capture, contradicting an initial assumption.

**Spike 3 — `preToolUse` `agent_message` on `deny` is a confirmed Cursor
bug.** First hard-block attempt: `preToolUse` returning
`{"permission": "deny", "agent_message": "..."}`. The tool call was
correctly blocked, but the model only ever saw a generic
"Rejected: Shell blocked by preToolUse hook" — never the custom message.
Cross-checked against Cursor's own docs/community forum, which confirmed
this as a known, staff-acknowledged bug (`agent_message` on `preToolUse`
deny, and `user_message` on `beforeSubmitPrompt`, don't reach the model).
This looked like it killed the hard-block design outright — downgraded to
an advisory `postToolUse` + `additional_context` approach (confirmed
working: reliably reaches the model as a `system_reminder`), at the cost of
never actually *preventing* the first violating action, only flagging it
after the fact.

**Spike 4 — reversal: `preToolUse` `user_message` (not `agent_message`)
*does* reach the model.** Re-tested systematically with a throwaway hook
emitting both fields side by side on the same deny. `agent_message`
confirmed still broken exactly as before; `user_message` — despite the same
docs/forum thread saying otherwise — reliably reached the model's context.
This directly contradicts the published documentation but was reproduced
consistently across repeated calls. Reinstated the hard-block design using
`permission: deny` + `user_message`, dropping `agent_message` entirely.
Also confirmed in the same spike: `postToolUse` never fires at all for a
call `preToolUse` already denied (no double-logging concern).

**Spike 5 — real latency required a subprocess-isolated hard watchdog, not
just a timeout kwarg.** `contradiction_resolution.resolve()` (existing,
already-validated module — `recall()` + Vertex-AI-backed
`check_contradiction()`) measured 5-10s typical with real credentials, but
both `recall()`'s HTTP client and `check_contradiction()`'s `litellm` call
have their own internal retry loops that are uncapped in aggregate — a
`preToolUse` hook blocking indefinitely on a hung network call would be far
worse than no check at all. Fix: the enforcer never calls `resolve()`
in-process; it always spawns a **separate child process**
(`hooks/_hindsight_check_worker.py`) via `subprocess.run(..., timeout=N)`
and treats `TimeoutExpired` as a normal fail-open path. Confirmed
empirically that this cleanly kills a genuinely hung child with no orphaned
process left behind.

**Spike 6 — `hindsight-api`'s `/recall` endpoint silently 400s past 500
tokens, which would have made the check a permanent silent no-op.**
Preflighting the false-positive rate against 12 real historical plan files
using their full plan-body text (up to ~3,000 chars / ~780 tokens) failed
every single `recall()` call with `HTTP 400`Bad Request` —
`spike/hindsight_client.py`'s own error handling swallows that into an
*empty* result list, which `resolve()` then correctly (from its own logic's
perspective) treats as "nothing to compare against, retain" — meaning the
enforcer would have run, cost real latency, and **never once actually
checked anything**, with no error surfaced anywhere. Fix: query
`recall()` with the plan's `overview` frontmatter field only (1-3 sentences
on every plan file inspected) — well under budget, and arguably the
*better* granularity anyway (stated intent, not implementation detail).

**Spike 7 — false-positive preflight surfaced a real design ambiguity, not
just a bug.** Re-ran the 12-plan sample with the corrected `overview`-only
query (read-only replica of `resolve()`'s decision logic, deliberately not
calling the real function to avoid writing to the live pending-contradiction
queue during a test): **1/12 (8.3%) flagged**, confidence 0.92, with a
semantically coherent explanation — the flagged plan introduced a new
structural guard that *intentionally* superseded an existing, more
permissive convention. This is the correct behavior of a contradiction
*detector*, but it exposed that `resolve()` has no way to distinguish "this
plan makes a genuine mistake" from "this plan deliberately, correctly
evolves the convention" — both look identical as a semantic conflict.
Resolved by making the marker's one-shot consumption an explicit design
choice rather than an implementation detail: the marker is deleted
**before** the check runs (crash-safe) and is never reinstated, so a deny is
a one-time speed bump per plan, not a permanent lock — the model sees the
explanation once, and a retry (same or revised action) passes through
cleanly via the "no marker" fast path.

**Spike 8 — `Task` subagents get a different `session_id`, a confirmed
coverage gap.** A throwaway `preToolUse` logging hook captured
`session_id` for a `Shell` call issued directly in the parent conversation
versus the same call issued through a `Task` subagent: completely different
IDs. Since the marker hand-off from `hooks/detect-plan-kickoff.sh` to
`hooks/post-plan-hindsight-check.py` is keyed by `session_id`, any plan
whose implementation is delegated to a subagent gets **zero** enforcement
coverage — not circumvention, just an unmatched lookup. Accepted as an
explicit scope limit for this pass rather than solved (would need a
`conversation_id`-based scheme instead, which trades away some of
`session_id`'s other useful properties).

**Implementation** (`hooks/` in this repo — see `docs/NEW_PROJECT_SETUP.md`
step 12 for the install procedure): `detect-plan-kickoff.sh`
(`beforeSubmitPrompt`) matches the boilerplate, extracts the newest plan
file's `overview`, detects the target project (`kubernaut`/`dcm`) from
`workspace_roots` by path prefix, writes a per-`session_id` marker.
`post-plan-hindsight-check.py` (`preToolUse`, matcher
`Write|StrReplace|Shell|EditNotebook`) consumes and immediately deletes the
marker, runs `_hindsight_check_worker.py` under a **45s** subprocess
watchdog, and denies with `user_message` only on `resolve()` returning
`auto_resolved`/`queued`. Every outcome (clean/denied/timeout/error/crash)
is logged to `~/.hindsight/logs/post-plan-hindsight-check.jsonl` for direct
false-positive-rate observability going forward, rather than inferring it
later from `reflect()` trends alone. 40 unit tests across
`tests/test_write_plan_marker.py`, `tests/test_hindsight_check_worker.py`,
and `tests/test_post_plan_hindsight_check.py` cover every fail-open branch,
the one-shot marker-consumption guarantee (including "worker crashes but
marker is still gone"), and that `agent_message` is never emitted (only
`user_message`) on a deny.

**Post-implementation smoke test found one more real bug the unit tests
couldn't catch: system `python3` lacks `litellm`/`vertexai`.** End-to-end
testing with real stdin/JSON (not mocks) against a live plan file initially
timed out — tracing it down showed the worker was being invoked with the
system `python3`, not `~/.hindsight/venv/bin/python3`, so
`check_contradiction()` failed immediately with
`No module named 'vertexai'` before ever reaching a real network call.
Fixed at the deployment layer: `hooks/install.sh` (the reusable installer,
see below) hardcodes the enforcer's `.cursor/hooks.json` command to
`~/.hindsight/venv/bin/python3`, never a bare `python3`. Re-testing with the
correct interpreter surfaced a second, more important finding: a genuine
successful `recall()` + `check_contradiction()` round trip through the
venv Python takes **13-17s** wall-clock in practice (interpreter startup +
one HTTP hop + one Vertex AI call) — well above the original 15s watchdog
budget picked from `resolve()`'s in-process latency numbers alone, meaning
roughly half of all real checks would have silently timed out and
fail-opened before ever completing. Raised `WATCHDOG_BUDGET_S` 15→45 based
on this measurement rather than the earlier estimate.

**Deployment**: `hooks/install.sh <target-repo>` — idempotent, JSON-merges
into any existing `.cursor/hooks.json` (`hooks/_merge_hooks_json.py`, 8
tests covering preservation of unrelated existing hooks and idempotency),
symlinks the four hook scripts into `~/.hindsight/hooks/` (same
single-source-of-truth rationale as `chunking.py`'s symlink into
`~/.hindsight/`, adapted for `hooks.json`'s absolute-path-reference model
rather than `chunking.py`'s Python-import model — the two aren't the same
mechanism, since hook scripts run from each target repo's own working
directory, not from a shared process). Installed into `kubernaut`,
`kubernaut-operator`, `kubernaut-console`, and all 13 `dcm-project/*`
repos. `.cursor/hooks.json` is gitignored the same way `.cursor/mcp.json`
already is (blanket `.cursor/*`, see the 2026-08-03 entry below) — it embeds
this machine's absolute paths and won't survive a fresh clone/worktree
without re-running the installer there.

**Known limitations, restated for anyone picking this up later**: (1) relies
on `user_message`'s undocumented-but-verified behavior — a future Cursor
release could silently change this without a deprecation notice, since it
isn't the documented mechanism; (2) the `Task`-subagent `session_id` gap
above; (3) `resolve()` detects semantic conflict, not author intent, so an
occasional deny on a deliberate, correct convention change is expected
behavior, not a bug — the one-shot retry path exists specifically to make
that cheap; (4) the false-positive rate (8.3% on a 12-plan sample) is a
preflight estimate, not a production baseline — the per-check JSONL log is
the mechanism for getting a real one after a week of live traffic; (5) this
pass covers `kubernaut`/`dcm` only, matching where the recurring-correction
problem was actually observed — `engram`'s own plan-kickoffs are
deliberately not gated yet.

**Also captured, not yet built** (see this repo's todo list, not filed as
GitHub issues — see the 2026-08-03 entry below for why this repo treats
`docs/findings/` as the retrospective record and issues as the
forward-looking backlog): wiring kubernaut's existing
`check-tdd-compliance.sh`/`check-business-integration.sh` into an
`afterFileEdit` hook; a `postToolUse` hook on `TodoWrite` flagging
RED/GREEN/REFACTOR phase-collapsing; a time-windowed `reflect()` call in
`nightly-learn.py` (today's only has an all-time window, which can't show
recurrence trends); and scoping what a deterministic structural check would
even mean for `dcm-project/*` repos, which have no equivalent scripts to
reuse today.

## 2026-08-04: Purged 177 Legacy Pre-`memory_id` Pending Contradictions — Unactionable Backlog, Not Data Loss

**Context**: the previous day's 24h status report flagged that kubernaut's
pending-contradictions count had grown 174→376 since the 2026-07-31 dedup fix
and confirmed the growth was genuine (zero duplicate `(new_statement,
memory_id)` pairs — see the entry below). That report also surfaced that 174
of those entries (177 once counted across all projects, not just kubernaut)
had `memory_id: null`. User asked to remove them.

**Why these were unactionable rather than just old**: `review-contradictions.py`'s
approve path needs `memory_id` (or `document_id`) to invalidate/supersede the
conflicting memory — its own code comments this exact case: *"No memory_id or
document_id on this entry (queued before the supersede fix) — old memory left
in place."* These 177 entries all predate the 2026-07-19 fix that started
threading `memory_id` through `pending_queue.append_pending()`, so a human
reviewer approving one of these would silently leave the old, contradicted
memory untouched — the review UI would look like it worked but wouldn't
actually resolve anything. Confirmed via a direct queue scan: all 177 are
timestamped between 2026-07-27 19:51 and 2026-07-31 12:54 UTC (the exact
window before that day's watermark/dedup fixes landed), spanning three
projects — kubernaut (173), dcm (3), engram (1).

**Fix**: backed up the live queue to
`contradictions-pending.jsonl.bak-legacy-purge-20260804-090826`, then filtered
out every entry with `memory_id is None` (390 → 213 entries: kubernaut
376→203, dcm 3→0, engram 1→0, koku's 10 untouched — koku onboarded well after
the memory_id fix, so none of its entries were affected). Regenerated
`docs/DASHBOARD.md` and `docs/PENDING_CONTRADICTIONS.md` via
`generate-dashboard.py` from the cleaned queue.

**Not done (and not needed)**: no fix to `pending_queue.py` itself — this was
a one-time historical cleanup of pre-fix data, not a recurring bug. Any entry
queued today already carries `memory_id` (confirmed live 2026-07-31, see
below), so this backlog of unactionable nulls cannot recur under current
code.

## 2026-08-03 (same day, third follow-up): `kubernaut-operator` Had No `.cursor/mcp.json` — Restored It, Then Verified It Survives Branch Switches

**Context**: the kubernaut-operator team reported no MCPs available in Cursor
and it was confirmed live. Global `~/.cursor/mcp.json` only carries the
shared `hindsight`/`gopls` servers by design (per
`docs/NEW_PROJECT_SETUP.md` step 8); project-specific servers
(`hindsight-docs`, `hindsight-issues`, `cocoindex-code`, `gopls`
project-scoped) come from each repo's own workspace-level
`.cursor/mcp.json`. `kubernaut-operator` simply had no such file — its
sibling repos (`kubernaut`, `kubernaut-v1.5`, `kubernaut-v1.6`,
`kubernaut-console`) all had one. Fixed by creating
`kubernaut-operator/.cursor/mcp.json` mirroring `kubernaut-v1.5`'s config.

**Follow-up question — will this survive branch switches in that repo?**
Checked `.gitignore`: `kubernaut-operator` blanket-ignores `.cursor/*` (with
`!.cursor/rules/`/`!.cursor/skills/` carved back out, no exception for
`mcp.json`) — same convention as `kubernaut`/`kubernaut-v1.5`/
`kubernaut-v1.6`. Verified empirically rather than just asserting it: `git
checkout main`, confirmed the file's md5 was unchanged, `git checkout` back
to the working feature branch, md5 still unchanged — `git checkout` only
adds/removes *tracked* files, so an untracked/ignored file is never touched
by an ordinary branch switch. `git worktree list` on that repo also showed
two other worktrees already in play (`/private/tmp/kubernaut-operator-v1.5.7`
and `~/.hindsight/watch/kubernaut-operator`, the branch-scoped CocoIndex
mirror from the entry below) — neither needs `mcp.json` since neither is
ever opened as a Cursor project.

**Why not just commit it (like `engram`/`kubernaut-console` do)?** Those two
repos are effectively single-machine/personal, so committing
`.cursor/mcp.json` is safe. `kubernaut-operator` has real outside
contributors, and the file embeds this machine's absolute paths
(`/Users/jgil/.hindsight/venv/bin/python3`, `/Users/jgil/go/bin/gopls`) —
committing it would push a broken config onto every other contributor's
checkout. Gitignoring it was the right call already made for
`kubernaut`/`kubernaut-v1.5`/`kubernaut-v1.6`; this just brought
`kubernaut-operator` in line.

**Residual gaps** (things branch-checkout safety does *not* cover):
`git clean -fdx` purges ignored files too (plain `-fd` does not), and a
fresh `git clone` or new `git worktree add` of the repo elsewhere starts
from tracked content only, so it won't have this file either. Documented
both the commit-vs-gitignore decision rule and this persistence boundary as
a gotcha in `docs/NEW_PROJECT_SETUP.md` step 7, so a future missing-file
report resolves by re-running that step instead of re-deriving the
root cause.

## 2026-08-03 (same day, second follow-up): Split `docs/FINDINGS.md` Into Monthly Files — Considered and Rejected a GitHub-Issues Migration

**Context**: this file had grown to 4,320 lines / 58 dated entries over 44
days, prepend-only, with no upper bound — exactly the risk flagged and left
unresolved in the 2026-07-31 "Follow-up idea (not yet acted on)" note below
(now in `docs/findings/2026-07.md`). Asked directly: how to reorganize for
long-term maintenance, whether to split into daily files, and whether to
replace the document with GitHub Issues (closing them and relying on
Engram's own recall instead of the file).

**Investigation**: two things made this lower-risk than it looked. First,
this file is already ingested into the live `engram-docs` Hindsight bank via
`engram-cocoindex-flows.py`'s `process_doc_file()`, using the
heading-anchored `chunking.split_markdown_sections()` shipped earlier today
(see the entry below) — recall-based historical search over these findings
already works today, independent of any file restructuring; issue
[#10](https://github.com/jordigilh/engram/issues/10)'s 7-case pilot
ground-truth benchmark is built directly from real content in this file and
proves it. Second, `grep`-ing every one of the 60+ files that cite
`docs/FINDINGS.md` (`report.py`, `generate-dashboard.py`,
`spike/ground_truth.py`, rule files, etc.) confirmed every single one is a
prose citation like *"see docs/FINDINGS.md 2026-07-08"* — zero code
anywhere does `open()`/`read_text()` on the file, so no runtime behavior
depends on its current single-file shape.

**Decision — monthly split, not daily, not GitHub Issues**:
- **Daily rejected**: entry cadence is too uneven (some days have 5+
  "follow-up" sub-entries, most days zero) — daily files would produce mostly
  empty files and no real reduction in per-file cognitive load on the busy
  days that actually matter.
- **GitHub Issues rejected**: `engram` deliberately runs the "no-issues-bank"
  variant of its own onboarding (`docs/NEW_PROJECT_SETUP.md`) — no
  `engram-issues` Hindsight bank, no CocoIndex `issues_app` for this repo,
  by design. `engram` *does* already use GitHub Issues today, but for a
  non-overlapping purpose: all 10 existing issues are `enhancement`-labeled
  forward-looking feature/design proposals (filed in one batch on
  2026-07-29), not incident postmortems. Most entries in this file are
  narrative root-cause writeups of decisions already made — a poor fit for
  an open/close ticket model — and standing up an issues-ingestion pipeline
  just for this file's content would reintroduce the exact kind of pipeline
  complexity this session spent the morning removing (see the chunk-ID and
  Postgres-pool entries below). GitHub Issues keeps doing exactly what it
  already does well: the forward-looking backlog.
- **Monthly chosen**: proportionate to actual cadence (a handful of files per
  quarter rather than one unbounded file, or dozens of near-empty daily
  ones). July 2026 alone is still ~3,500 lines / 46 entries since that's when
  most of this project's work happened, but it is now a closed, frozen
  archive rather than a perpetually-growing one — only the current month's
  file grows.

**Implementation**: wrote a one-time migration script (not committed) that
split the 58 entries by their `## YYYY-MM-DD` headers into
`docs/findings/2026-06.md`, `docs/findings/2026-07.md`, and this file,
preserving exact byte-for-byte body content and the existing newest-first
ordering; the script asserted the reconstructed entries equal the original
file's body before writing anything. `docs/FINDINGS.md` itself becomes a
short index with a reverse-chronological, linked table of contents into
these files — same stable path, so the 60+ existing `"see docs/FINDINGS.md
DATE"` citations elsewhere in the codebase stay resolvable (one hop through
the index) without needing to be rewritten. The CocoIndex docs matcher
(`path_matcher=PatternFilePathMatcher(included_patterns=["**/*.md"])`)
already recurses, so `docs/findings/*.md` is picked up with no pipeline code
changes.

**Verification**: the migration script's own reconciliation check passed
(reconstructed entries == original body, byte-for-byte); full `pytest` run
afterward confirmed no regressions (no code changed, docs-only).
