## 2026-08-03 (same day, follow-up): Every `*-cocoindex-flows.py` Was Holding a Default-Sized 10-Connection Postgres Pool It Never Needed

**Context**: user noticed "a lot of postgres running instances" after the
engram service restart above and asked whether they were actually needed.
`ps aux | grep postgres` showed only **one** real `postgres` server process
(plus its normal fixed set of background workers: checkpointer, walwriter,
autovacuum launcher, logical replication launcher, 3 io workers, logger) —
so "instances" in the visual sense was a red herring; the real question was
the ~18 separate `postgres: hindsight hindsight 127.0.0.1(port) idle` client
backend rows underneath it. Cross-checked against `pg_stat_activity` directly
(`max_connections=100`, 28 total connections counted, 20 against the
`hindsight` database) rather than guessing from `ps` output alone.

**Root cause**: every `*-cocoindex-flows.py` calls `postgres.create_pool(PG_DSN)`
with **no `min_size`/`max_size` kwargs** for its `code_app`'s pgvector
`PG_POOL` — confirmed `cocoindex.connectors.postgres.create_pool()` is a thin
pass-through to `asyncpg.create_pool()`, whose own library defaults are
`min_size=10, max_size=10`. Since `min_size == max_size` by default, each
pool isn't elastic at all — it eagerly holds exactly 10 connections open for
the lifetime of the process, regardless of how often `code_app` actually
touches Postgres (in practice: rare, bursty pgvector upserts triggered only
by real file changes, not sustained concurrent traffic). Two processes were
running live at the time (`io.vectorize.cocoindex.service` for kubernaut,
`io.vectorize.cocoindex.engram`), so that's up to 20 connections held open
just for two lightly-used pools — and this multiplies by one more full
10-connection pool for every additional project onboarded (`koku`, `dcm`)
once their flows run in live mode, none of which was ever deliberately
decided, just inherited from never overriding the constructor's defaults.

**Fix — right-sized, not eliminated**, following the exact same philosophy
already applied to `hindsight-api`'s own pool on 2026-08-02 (re-derive a
number from actual observed usage rather than accepting or blindly
multiplying a vendor default): added `PG_POOL_MIN_SIZE`/`PG_POOL_MAX_SIZE`
module constants (env-overridable via `COCOINDEX_PG_POOL_MIN_SIZE`/
`COCOINDEX_PG_POOL_MAX_SIZE`, defaulting to **2/5**) to all four
`*-cocoindex-flows.py` files, passed into their `create_pool()` calls.
2 keeps a small warm floor (avoids reconnect latency on the next write); 5
still allows real parallelism during an initial full-repo backfill walk,
just not 2x more than that walk realistically drives concurrently. Made the
new env vars explicit in the `service`/`engram`/`koku` launchd plists
alongside their existing (already-explicit-despite-matching-the-code-default)
`COCOINDEX_PG_URL` entry, for the same discoverability reason — `dcm.plist`
was left alone since it doesn't set `COCOINDEX_PG_URL` either (it runs
`dcm-cocoindex-flows.py` as a one-shot nightly `StartCalendarInterval` batch
job via `dcm-nightly-ingest.sh`, not a `KeepAlive` live daemon, so it was
never actually contributing to the steady-state connection count in the
first place — confirmed by cross-checking its plist rather than assuming).

**Verified before/after**: full test suite still 351/352 passing (same
pre-existing, unrelated `check-rule-sync` failure as the entry above) after
the change. Restarted both currently-live services
(`io.vectorize.cocoindex.service`, `io.vectorize.cocoindex.engram`) to pick
up the new pool sizing immediately rather than waiting for their next
natural restart.

**Reconciled every connection after restart, not just the rough count.**
Post-restart, `pg_stat_activity` still showed ~20 total connections to
`hindsight` — barely changed from before, which could have looked like the
fix did nothing. Split by `backend_start` relative to the restart instant:
exactly **4** connections dated from the restart itself (two near-identical
timestamps 12:44:04.554/.555 and 12:44:04.575/.576 — `min_size=2` firing for
each of the two pools, precisely as configured, down from 10+10=20 before),
and the other **15** all predated the restart and were untouched by it —
tracked to PID 28480 (`hindsight-api`, blue, running since 1:00 AM, 66% CPU
at the time), sitting exactly at its own already-deliberately-tuned
`HINDSIGHT_API_DB_POOL_MAX_SIZE=15` ceiling while its background worker
actively churned through `seed_sources`/`victims` batch-claim queries. So
the two pools this fix targeted dropped **80%** (20→4 connections) exactly
as designed; the apparently-unchanged total was `hindsight-api`'s own
pool — a different, already-addressed area (2026-08-02) — coincidentally
running near-saturated with legitimate background work at the exact moment
of the before/after comparison, not a sign the fix underperformed.

## 2026-08-03: The "Still-Open" `kubernaut-issues` Redundancy Root Cause — Positional Chunk IDs Cascading on Every Prepend/Comment, Not `hindsight-api`'s Delta-Diff

**Context**: direct follow-up to the branch-switching-churn entry below, which
explicitly flagged `kubernaut-issues`' redundancy as a **different, still-open**
root cause (line ~130 of this file, same day) and floated two possible fixes:
registering a real `"exact"` strategy in `hindsight-api`, or fixing its
delta-diff. Investigated `hindsight-api` (a third-party PyPI package, not
this repo's source) to act on the first option and found `strategy: "exact"`
was never registered *client-side* either — every `hindsight_retain()` call
across all four `*-cocoindex-flows.py` files sent a `"strategy": "exact"`
field that `hindsight-api` silently ignores (confirmed against its OpenAPI
spec: no such field exists on the retain-items schema). It was dead code,
not a broken feature — there was nothing to "register."

**Real root cause — cascading positional chunk IDs, not `hindsight-api` at
all.** `_split_text()` in every flow file chunked docs/issues by fixed
character offset and derived `document_id`s from the chunk's *index*
(`{base_id}--chunk{i}`). Any edit before a given offset — prepending a new
`## Section` to a markdown file, or a new comment landing on an issue/PR —
shifts every downstream chunk's boundaries, so chunk `i`'s *content* is now
different from what chunk `i` was last time, even though the actual prose
in that chunk never changed. `hindsight-api` correctly sees "different
content at this `document_id`" and does a full re-extraction — this was
never a delta-diff bug, it was engram feeding it a legitimately-different
payload every time. `docs/FINDINGS.md` itself (edited almost exclusively by
prepending new dated entries at the top, exactly like this edit) and any
issue/PR that accumulates comments over time are worst-case inputs for this
pattern — which lines up with `kubernaut-issues`' measured ~4x redundancy
(60,762 units / 15,226 distinct `document_id`s, previous entry) far better
than a `hindsight-api` delta-diff bug would.

**Fix — content-anchored chunk IDs, not positional ones, plus dropping the
dead field.** New shared module `chunking.py`, imported by all four
`*-cocoindex-flows.py` (symlinked into `~/.hindsight/chunking.py`, see
`docs/INSTALL.md` step 16):

- `split_fixed_window()` — the original offset-based splitter verbatim,
  kept as the fallback for oversized sections that have no better anchor.
- `split_markdown_sections()` — splits on `^#{1,6} ` heading boundaries
  instead of raw offsets, keyed by a short sha1 digest of the heading line
  itself (stable across prepend/reorder), falling back to
  `split_fixed_window()`-style numbering only for oversized content within
  one section.
- `split_issue_sections()` — one section for the static header+description,
  then one section per comment keyed by the comment's **ordinal position in
  the already-filtered comment list** (`comment{i}`), not by character
  offset into a concatenated string — so a new comment #6 never touches
  comments #1-5's `document_id`s or content.
- Every flow's issue/PR/Jira-issue formatter was also split into a static
  header (title/author/created/description) and per-comment sections, with
  volatile fields (`state`, `labels`) deliberately **excluded** from the
  chunked header — they're already carried in `metadata`/`tags`, which
  `hindsight-api` can update without a content-hash change. Previously
  every state transition (e.g. `OPEN`→`CLOSED`) or label edit silently
  rewrote the *entire* issue body's content, forcing full re-extraction of
  unrelated description text along with it.
- Removed the dead `strategy: "exact"` field entirely rather than replacing
  it with `hindsight-api`'s real `retain_extraction_mode: "verbatim"`/
  `"chunks"` options — both were evaluated and rejected: `"verbatim"` still
  calls an LLM (just for metadata, not synthesis) so it wouldn't have
  addressed the redundant-Sonnet-consolidation cost either; `"chunks"`
  skips LLM extraction entirely but trades away fact-synthesis recall
  quality repo-wide, a bigger tradeoff than this fix required.

**Test coverage** — `tests/test_chunking.py` (new) plus regression tests
added to each flow's existing test file (`test_cocoindex_flows.py`,
`test_engram_cocoindex_flows.py`, `test_koku_cocoindex_flows.py`, and new
`test_dcm_cocoindex_flows.py`) asserting: prepending a section leaves
earlier sections' `document_id`/content untouched, appending a comment
leaves earlier comments' `document_id`/content untouched, a state/label
change leaves the header's `document_id`/content untouched, and the
`strategy` field is absent from the retain payload. Full suite run:
352 tests, 351 passed — the 1 failure (`test_check_rule_sync.py`'s
four-pairs assertion, pre-existing since the koku-onboarding commit
earlier the same day) is unrelated and reproduces identically on a clean
`git stash`.

**Fixed `dcm-cocoindex-flows.py`'s `PG_POOL` `ContextKey` collision as a
side effect of getting it under test** — it shared the literal string
`"pg_pool"` with `cocoindex-flows.py`'s own `ContextKey`, which only
explodes with `ValueError` once both modules load into the *same* process
(e.g. pytest collecting both test files together) — renamed to the
per-file-unique `"dcm_repo_pg_pool"`, matching the pattern
`engram-cocoindex-flows.py`/`koku-cocoindex-flows.py` already used.

**Timing**: deployed before the previous entry's 3:00 AM
`cleanup-kubernaut-docs-issues.sh` one-shot ran, specifically so
`kubernaut-docs`/`kubernaut-issues`' from-scratch backfill would build its
initial `document_id` set using the new stable scheme rather than the old
positional one — avoiding a second full re-backfill later just to pick up
this fix.

## 2026-08-03: Runaway CocoIndex Re-ingestion From Branch-Switching Churn — Live Dev Clones Replaced With Branch-Scoped Mirrors

**Context**: woke up to `kubernaut-docs` unconsolidated memory units spiking
7,594 → 43,972 in 15 minutes, `kubernaut-issues` showing the same continuous
churn, and `hindsight-stdout-blue.log` full of `Extract facts:` / `no
unchanged chunks... falling back to full retain` lines — each one paying a
real Vertex AI Sonnet consolidation call. Immediately stopped all three
kubernaut-family/dcm/engram CocoIndex `launchd` jobs (`io.vectorize.
cocoindex.service`, `.engram`, `.dcm`) via `launchctl bootout` to cut off new
spend while root-causing.

**Where Sonnet actually sits in the pipeline** (came up mid-investigation,
worth recording since it shaped the fix): `cocoindex-flows.py`'s own
`process_doc_file`/`process_issue` never call an LLM directly — they do plain
character-based chunking (`_split_text`) and push raw text to
`hindsight_retain()` over HTTP. Every `slow llm call` line in
`hindsight-stdout-blue.log` is tagged `scope=consolidation,
model=litellm/vertex_ai/claude-sonnet-4-6` — Sonnet is invoked **server-side,
inside `hindsight-api`**, exclusively for the background consolidation worker
that merges/dedupes/synthesizes raw retained chunks into curated
observations and mental models. Retain-time fact extraction is a separate,
much faster call (~3.4s for one chunk vs. 15-70s for a consolidation batch),
consistent with a lighter model. So the cost is structural: anything that
causes redundant re-`retain()`s of unchanged content pays for redundant
Sonnet consolidation on top, even though CocoIndex itself never touches an
LLM for docs/issues.

**Root cause — traced via exact file-count matching, not inference.** Found
`docs/architecture/decisions/DD-AUDIT-003-...md` had an mtime of 06:15 AM
today despite `git status` being clean and the last real commit dated
2026-08-02 20:16 — the file's *content* hadn't changed, only its mtime.
`find docs -name "*.md" -newermt "06:00" ! -newermt "06:30" | wc -l` returned
**135** — the exact same count as the "falling back to full retain" events
logged in that window. `git reflog` for the live kubernaut clone showed why:

```
06:15:41 checkout: moving from fix/1861-vertex-ai-explicit-credentials to main
06:15:42 pull origin main --quiet: Fast-forward
06:18:44 checkout: moving from main to fix/1869-sre-persona-approval-visibility-rbac
06:20:19 commit: fix(apifrontend): ... (#1869)
```

Routine automated branch-switching (PR work) touches the mtime of every file
that differs between whichever two branches were involved in each checkout —
`cocoindex-flows.py`'s `docs_app`/`code_app` used
`localfs.walk_dir(..., live=True)` pointed directly at the **live, actively
branch-switched dev clones** (`~/go/src/github.com/jordigilh/kubernaut`,
`-operator`, `-console`, `-demo-scenarios`, `-docs`). CocoIndex correctly
detected the touched mtimes as "changed" and reprocessed them, but the delta
was branch-switching noise, not real content evolution — a `strategy:
"exact"` no-op-if-unchanged flag in `hindsight_retain()` calls was also
silently never registered in `hindsight-api`'s config, so every one of these
resubmissions fell all the way through to a full delta-diff and, per the
`no unchanged chunks` log line, `hindsight-api`'s own diff also failed to
recognize the resubmitted content as identical.

**Fix — stop watching the live checkout entirely, watch dedicated
branch-scoped mirrors instead.** Checked `cocoindex/connectors/` first: no
native git-ref source exists, only `localfs`/`postgres`/`surrealdb`. So the
fix is at the git level, not CocoIndex's:

- `watch-mirrors-config.sh` — single source of truth, a `name|live_clone|
  branch|mirror_path` array. 6 entries: `kubernaut`, `kubernaut-operator`,
  `kubernaut-console`, `kubernaut-demo-scenarios`, `kubernaut-docs` (all
  `main` — `release/v1.5` was considered and explicitly dropped: it's
  diverged enough that `main` is now authoritative, and other repos' release
  branches are already covered via issue tracking), plus `engram` itself.
- `watch-mirrors-lib.sh` — `ensure_mirror()`: `git fetch origin <branch>`,
  then either `git worktree add --detach <mirror_path> origin/<branch>`
  (first run) or `git -C <mirror_path> reset --hard origin/<branch>`
  (subsequent runs). Sourced by both `setup-watch-mirrors.sh` (one-time
  bootstrap) and `refresh-watch-mirrors.sh` (periodic sync, new
  `io.vectorize.cocoindex.watch-sync.plist`, every 600s) so they can't drift
  apart.
- **Why `reset --hard` on a single branch actually fixes the "unknown
  delta" problem, not just relocates it**: `git checkout` between two
  *divergent* branches touches the mtime of every file that differs between
  them — which can be huge and has nothing to do with real content
  evolution. `git reset --hard` moving *forward along one branch's linear
  history* only rewrites the mtime of files whose blob actually changed in
  the new commits. Watching mirrors that only ever fast-forward `main` turns
  "file changed" back into a real, trustworthy content signal, by
  construction — no CocoIndex-side patching needed.
- `ENGRAM_DOCS_DIR`/`ENGRAM_CODE_DIR`/`ENGRAM_OPERATOR_DIR`/
  `ENGRAM_CONSOLE_DIR`/`ENGRAM_SCENARIOS_DIR` (in `io.vectorize.cocoindex.
  service.plist`) and `ENGRAM_REPO_DIR` (in `io.vectorize.cocoindex.
  engram.plist`) now point at `~/.hindsight/watch/<repo>` instead of the
  live clones — and the same paths were made the *default* fallback in
  `cocoindex-flows.py`/`engram-cocoindex-flows.py` (previously only the
  plists overrode the unsafe live-clone defaults; a manual `python3
  cocoindex-flows.py` invocation would have silently used them).
- `code_app` was brought into the same fix, not just `docs_app`, despite
  costing no LLM calls: `code_embeddings` turned out to have zero true
  duplicate `(filepath, chunk_index)` rows (real primary key, clean
  upserts — the largest per-file chunk counts, e.g. 1,919 for
  `oas_json_gen.go`, are legitimate: that generated file is 1.29MB/50,534
  lines, verified against the live checkout), so the risk there was never
  storage bloat — it was `engram_code_search` relevance silently drifting
  toward whichever feature branch happened to be checked out at the last
  reprocessing, not "current main."
- **`engram` itself was triaged, not just assumed safe** because it's
  smaller: `engram-cocoindex-flows.py` has the structurally identical
  `walk_dir(ENGRAM_REPO_DIR, live=True)` pattern. It shows zero symptom
  today only because its `git reflog` has zero branch checkouts (`commit:`/
  `reset:`/`commit (amend)` only — engram is worked on via direct commits to
  `main`) and zero `falling back to full retain` occurrences in its logs.
  That safety is behavioral, not architectural, and will evaporate the
  moment engram's own workflow adopts feature branches — so it was added to
  `watch-mirrors-config.sh` now, as a zero-cost future-proof, rather than
  deferred.
- **Verified the actual fix, not just the restart**: after redeploying,
  checked out `main` and back to the original feature branch in the *live*
  kubernaut clone (the exact churn pattern that caused this morning's
  incident) and confirmed zero new `docs_app`/`code_app` activity in
  `cocoindex-stderr.log` — only the unrelated scheduled issues-poll. The
  mirror at `~/.hindsight/watch/kubernaut` never moved.

**Database size, measured directly (not guessed) — confirms months of this
churn already landed in the corpus.** Hindsight's own tables total **~9.2GB**:
`memory_links` 6534MB (dominant — relationship/co-occurrence edges scale with
every duplicate memory unit, compounding faster than linear), `memory_units`
2032MB, `llm_requests` 220MB, `unit_entities` 142MB, `documents` 131MB,
`chunks` 119MB, `entity_cooccurrences` 72MB. `kubernaut-docs`: 142,178 memory
units for only 40,406 distinct `document_id`s (~3.5x redundancy).
`kubernaut-issues`: 60,762 units for 15,226 distinct `document_id`s (~4x).
Note `kubernaut-issues`' redundancy has a **different, still-open** root
cause than docs: `issues_app` polls the GitHub API every 300s, never touches
local filesystem state, so it was never subject to the branch-churn bug
above — its `process_issue` is already `@coco.fn(memo=True)`, so the
redundancy most likely comes from `hindsight-api`'s same broken
"no-unchanged-chunks → full retain" fallback firing on *genuinely* updated
issues/PRs (new comments, label/state changes) over the weeks this pipeline
has run, not from CocoIndex resubmitting identical content. The mirror fix
does not address this — flagged as a follow-up (registering a real `"exact"`
strategy in `hindsight-api`, or fixing its delta-diff, would).

**Cleanup scheduled off-hours, not run immediately.** Clearing
`kubernaut-docs`/`kubernaut-issues` (`DELETE /v1/default/banks/{bank_id}/
memories` — confirmed via `hindsight-api`'s own OpenAPI spec, backs
`clear_memories`) does *not* by itself cause CocoIndex to resubmit anything:
`@coco.fn(memo=True)` memoization in `cocoindex.db` tracks "already
processed" independently of `hindsight-api`'s state, so a cleared bank stays
empty until `cocoindex.db` is also reset. That's safe for `code_app`
(idempotent `pgvector` upserts) and `transcript_app` (protected by its own
separate watermark file, see 2026-07-31 entry — unaffected by resetting
`cocoindex.db`), but for `docs`/`issues` a real reset means re-extracting
~55K documents from scratch, which empties both banks for the duration
(potentially hours) — and both are on kubernaut's *mandatory* recall list.
Rather than run this mid-morning, wrote `cleanup-kubernaut-docs-issues.sh`
(stop the service, clear both banks, back up and reset `cocoindex.db`, run
`cocoindex-flows.py --mode backfill --apps docs issues`, restart the
service) and scheduled it via a new one-shot `io.vectorize.cocoindex.
cleanup-once.plist` (`StartCalendarInterval` 3:00 AM) that self-removes
(`launchctl bootout` + `rm` its own plist) after running once, so it never
fires a second night.

## 2026-08-02: Zero-Downtime Blue/Green Restart for `hindsight-api` — And a Real Connection-Leak Bug Found Along the Way

**Context**: direct follow-up to 2026-07-07's "Cursor shows the MCP as down
every morning" (not fully solved) and the intervening work to move the
nightly restart from a stale-cached 3pm/2pm trigger to a clean 1am
(`UserEventAgent` was holding a stale calendar-trigger cache; killing and
letting it respawn fixed the schedule itself — separate from this entry).
Fixing *when* the restart happens didn't fix the actual complaint: whenever
it fires, Cursor's HTTP MCP client to `hindsight`/`hindsight-docs`/
`hindsight-issues` (all served by the same `hindsight-api` process on
`:8888`) drops and never auto-retries, staying disabled until a manual
reload — regardless of the hour. Before building anything, checked whether
the restart was even still *necessary* at the current heap level: 11h into
that day's uptime, RSS was 410MB (vs. the 17GB crisis and even the 1GB
post-fix baseline from 2026-06-26) — strong evidence the GPU/pool-size fixes
from that entry already did the heavy lifting and the *blind daily* restart
is overkill most days. User's call: keep the schedule as-is, but make the
restart's *impact* zero instead of reducing its frequency.

**Fix, part 1 — architecture**: `hindsight-api` no longer binds `:8888`
directly. It now runs as a **blue/green pair** (`io.vectorize.hindsight.
service-{blue,green}.plist`, internal ports 18888/18889, exactly one loaded
at a time) behind `hindsight-proxy.py` — a ~90-line stdlib-only asyncio TCP
proxy that is the *only* thing that ever binds `:8888` and is never touched
by the restart job. `hindsight-blue-green-restart.sh` (invoked by
`io.vectorize.hindsight.restart.plist` in place of the old `pkill -f
hindsight-api`) does a real make-before-break swap: start a fresh instance
on the standby color, wait for it to pass a readiness gate, flip the proxy's
active-backend state file (`~/.hindsight/state/active-backend.port`, re-read
per new TCP connection), drain the old instance for 10s, tear it down. Port
8888 never refuses a connection at any point.

**Fix, part 2 — the readiness gate had to be stricter than "`/health` says
200"**: first live test looked broken — port 8888 (and the backend
*directly*, bypassing the proxy entirely) went fully unresponsive for
60-70s, well after the swap script had already declared victory and moved
on. Root cause was **not** the proxy: `hindsight-api` passes `/health`
almost immediately on startup, then its in-process worker claims a startup
burst of recovered/pending tasks up to `HINDSIGHT_API_WORKER_MAX_SLOTS`
(default 10) — and because no `HINDSIGHT_API_READ_DATABASE_URL` is
configured (single embedded Postgres, no read replica), there is only **one**
shared DB connection pool for everything: worker tasks *and* HTTP-serving
recall/health requests. A worker claiming its full default of 10 tasks
against a pool sized 10 (the 2026-06-26 fix) can claim every connection,
starving incoming HTTP requests until a task completes. `/health` itself
is cheap enough to occasionally slip through fast, which is why a single
200 wasn't a reliable readiness signal. **Fix**: the swap script now
requires 5 *consecutive* checks that both succeed and complete within 2s
before declaring the standby ready (`HEALTH_TIMEOUT_S` raised 90→180s to
give this room) — a slow/queued check resets the counter to zero.

**Fix, part 3 — tuned the actual contention source**: bumped
`HINDSIGHT_API_DB_POOL_MAX_SIZE` 10→15 and added
`HINDSIGHT_API_WORKER_MAX_SLOTS=5` (previously unset, defaulting to 10) to
`config.env`/`config.env.example`. 15 was picked deliberately over reverting
to upstream's default of 100 — that default was directly responsible for
6.6GB of the original 17GB heap leak (2026-06-26), and this repo already
has a documented instance of "restore the old default" being the wrong
instinct for this exact setting. 5 worker slots leaves ≥10 of 15 connections
free for HTTP even at the worker's own claimed maximum. Confirmed via the
worker's own `[WORKER_STATS]` log line before/after: `slots=10/10 ...
pool: size=10 ... pending_acquires=37` (saturated) →
`slots=5/5 ... pool: size=15 ... pending_acquires=0` (headroom) on a fresh
restart under equivalent load.

**Bug found along the way (candidate for upstream report, alongside the
still-open #2529/#2534 from 2026-07-02/07-07)**: a Postgres backend was
found `idle in transaction` for **19+ minutes**, permanently pinning one
pool slot until the owning `hindsight-api` process was killed. Traced to a
`graph_maintenance` task that failed with an `asyncpg` `TimeoutError`
(`claim_graph_maintenance_batch` → `conn.fetch` → `bind_execute`) — the
failure path did not roll back or release the connection. Each occurrence
permanently shrinks the *effective* pool size until the process restarts,
compounding any contention rather than just being a one-off slow query. Not
yet filed upstream; worth reproducing in isolation and reporting if it
recurs outside of artificially heavy load (see next finding for how it was
triggered here).

**Methodology lesson — the first "outage" during testing was self-inflicted,
and it briefly looked exactly like a production bug.** Verifying the swap
with a tight-loop curl monitor (sub-second spacing, dozens of requests in
under two minutes, one run's monitor still finishing while the next test
already started) drove `pending_acquires` far higher than any plausible
real Cursor traffic pattern would, and combined with the connection-leak bug
above to produce a genuine multi-minute full outage — which was easy to
mis-read as "the proxy is broken" before checking `pg_stat_activity`
directly. Switching to *gently paced* verification (single requests, 2-3s
apart, one test run allowed to fully settle before starting the next) both
reproduced the real, worth-fixing readiness-gate gap **and** cleanly
confirmed zero downtime once the fixes above were in place — 135s of
3s-spaced health checks spanning an entire live swap, zero failures.

**Docs updated**: `docs/README.md` (architecture diagram + components
table), `docs/INSTALL.md` (install steps 5/troubleshooting/upgrading/
uninstall), `docs/COCOINDEX.md` (restart command in its pg0 troubleshooting
section) — all previous single-`io.vectorize.hindsight.service.plist`
references replaced with the blue/green + proxy equivalents.

**Takeaways**:
- **A liveness/health check answering fast on its own is not the same
  question as "can this instance absorb real traffic right now."** A
  process can be honestly healthy (DB reachable, app initialized) and still
  be functionally saturated moments later by its *own* background work
  competing for the same finite resource (here: one DB connection pool
  shared between a startup task-claim burst and user-facing requests, with
  no read/write pool separation configured). Readiness gates for any
  make-before-break swap need to probe for sustained responsiveness, not a
  single passing check.
- **When a fix moved a setting away from a vendor default for a documented
  reason (here: pool size, to fix a heap leak), don't casually 10x it back
  toward that default to solve a new, different problem.** Re-derive a
  number from the *current* evidence (the worker's own claimed-task ceiling)
  instead — got the contention relief without reintroducing the leak.
- **Synthetic load-testing a shared, stateful backend without deliberate
  recovery time between runs can manufacture an outage that looks identical
  to a real one.** Always check the datastore's own session/lock view
  (`pg_stat_activity` here) before concluding a symptom is in the layer
  you're actively testing (the proxy) rather than a downstream one.
- **A repeated, silently-inconsistent test harness (interrupting a
  multi-step script mid-flight) can leave *its own* infrastructure in a
  half-migrated state** — an interrupted swap attempt left a bootstrapped
  standby instance behind that caused an unrelated-looking `launchctl
  bootstrap` `Input/output error` on the next attempt. Worth explicitly
  reconciling to a known-clean state after any interrupted test, not just
  retrying.

> **Follow-up idea (not yet acted on)**: this file is now 55 dated entries /
> ~4,000 lines spanning 2026-06-11 to today, all in one ever-growing
> markdown file. Worth revisiting the format at some point — e.g. a
> daily/weekly-log split (one file per period, this file becoming an index
> or just the most recent window) instead of a single flat append-only
> document — so it doesn't keep growing indefinitely and cost more
> tokens/attention to search or recall from than it needs to. No action
> taken yet; flagging so the idea isn't lost.

## 2026-08-08 (same day, third follow-up): Investigated the Remaining 29 Entries via Three Parallel Subagents + Direct Checks — 40 → 6, Only Two Genuinely Undecided in Code Plus Four Low-Confidence Preflights

**Context**: continuation of the same-day bulk-triage → judgment-cluster →
leftover-8 chain above. Of the 40 entries remaining after the second
follow-up, the user chose to preflight all but two explicit exceptions
(`#18` RCA git-bisect, `#39` strategic milestone call). Rather than working
through 29 entries serially, three subagents were dispatched in parallel by
repo/theme — `kubernaut-demo-scenarios` disputes (6 entries), the
`HAPI`-vs-`KA` legacy-naming cluster (5 entries), and core `kubernaut` Go
service disputes (16 entries) — while 9 entries were checked directly
(GitHub PRs/issues, live cluster state, kubeconfig files, a GA-readiness
canvas, quay.io/skopeo, docs greps).

**Outcome — every entry got a real, code-grounded answer except six**:
- **15 entries**: existing memory was wrong per current code/docs;
  invalidated and replaced with the current, evidence-backed fact.
- **19 entries**: existing memory or the dispute itself was moot, stale,
  already-superseded, or a one-off session snapshot with no lasting
  behavioral claim worth keeping; removed without a replacement retain.
- **4 standalone facts** retained that didn't map 1:1 to a single disputed
  entry (FMC E2E cross-cluster hybrid state, KA's MCP-client status being
  `Proposed`/unimplemented, DataStorage's reject-not-supersede duplicate
  policy, and `RemediationWorkflow.component` now being `[]string`).
- **6 entries left untouched**, all needing either the two explicitly
  skipped items or content that came back genuinely unresolved from
  investigation rather than just uninvestigated:
  - `#13` shared-cluster helm-upgrade policy — no doc found either way.
  - `#18` RCA git-bisect — skipped by request.
  - `#24`/`#25` VHS tape playback speed — configs are byte-identical
    between the two tapes in dispute; only watching the actual recordings
    can resolve it.
  - `#32` `SignalType`/`SignalName` naming in `AIAnalysis` RCA fields — the
    *only* entry where the subagent's own verdict was "still genuinely
    undecided in the code" (the field is still named `SignalType`, and
    there's no evidence either the rename or the "leave it" call was ever
    made a real decision) — a legitimate open design question, not a
    preflight gap.
  - `#39` strategic milestone call — skipped by request.

**Notable individual findings** (see subagent transcripts for full detail,
linked from the chat):
- **HAPI is completely gone, not just deprecated**: the Python
  `holmesgpt-api` source was deleted 2026-04-25 (PR #843) after an earlier
  accidental reintroduction from a stale branch merge; it never reached GA
  and was fully replaced by KA (the Go rewrite). This alone mooted 3 of the
  5 entries in that cluster outright.
- **Two disputes each turned out to have a third, unconsidered answer** —
  neither side of the original argument was right:
  - `WithLogger`/`StartInteractiveSession` (KA) had been described in one
    session as "still uncommitted" and in another as "on a different
    branch" — they were actually already merged to `main` weeks earlier
    (commits `96fc3d000`/`c9179db90`, 2026-05-26).
  - AF's duplicate-`RemediationRequest` detection had been argued as
    "still label-based" vs. "needs migrating to field selectors" — current
    code does neither: it's a deterministic-name `Get` (`is-<RRName>`), a
    third approach that sidesteps the field-selector-vs-label debate
    entirely.
- **`RemediationWorkflow.spec.labels.component` really did become an
  array**: issue #790 (closed) and the current CRD schema (`type: array`)
  confirm the earlier "just use a temporary wildcard `*`" workaround was
  superseded once the schema fix landed — this reverses what a plain
  `gh issue` search on a *different*, similarly-named field (`Priority` in
  a DataStorage-internal label-matching struct, issue #599) had suggested
  in an earlier direct check on this same entry; the subagent's
  file-and-line match to the actual disputed CRD field is authoritative.
- **A "fix pushed" memory can go stale from architecture changes, not just
  reverts**: KA's nil-guarded `AnomalyDetector.Reset()` fix no longer exists
  in the code — not because it was ripped out, but because a later refactor
  made the detector un-nil-able by construction
  (`anomalyDetectorFor(correlationID)` always returns a live instance).
  Neither original side of that dispute ("silent skip" vs. "should panic")
  anticipated "make the nil state unreachable instead."

**Execution**: `/tmp/execute_final_40.py` (dry-run validated against the
live queue first, then `--execute`) — same `invalidate_memory` +
`hindsight_retain` + `remove_pending` shape as the two prior passes. 4 of
the 15 `invalidate_memory` calls returned the same benign "already gone"
(404) result seen in every prior pass (auto-consolidation beat the script
to it); all 15 `hindsight_retain` calls succeeded, so no information was
lost. Logged to
`~/.hindsight/logs/final-40-resolution-2026-08-08.jsonl`.

**Queue trajectory today**: 219 → 72 (bulk triage) → 48 (13 judgment
clusters) → 40 (8 leftovers) → **6** (this pass) — a 97% reduction from the
original backlog, with the 6 remaining entries being genuinely
un-preflightable (skipped-by-request, config-identical-so-needs-a-human-eye,
or a real open design question) rather than un-investigated.

**Takeaway**: dispatching parallel subagents scoped by repo/theme (rather
than working 29 entries serially) let each one build focused context on
one codebase area and cross-check multiple related entries against the
same files, which is likely *why* it surfaced two "neither side was right"
resolutions that a single serial pass checking one fact at a time might
have missed — clustering by investigation *surface*, not just by dispute
theme, seems to pay off similarly to the earlier semantic clustering of the
raw complaints.

## 2026-08-08 (same day, fourth follow-up): Closed the Final 6 With User Ground Truth + Two Targeted Investigations — 6 → 0, Pending Contradictions Backlog Fully Cleared

**Context**: the last 6 entries left after the third follow-up above — the
two the user explicitly skipped (`#18`, `#39`), two VHS-tape-speed entries
the user couldn't resolve from memory (`#24`/`#25`), one policy question
with no doc either way (`#13`), and one field-naming dispute that
investigation had confirmed was genuinely still open in the code (`#32`).
Presented all 6 in full (not just as tracking numbers) so the user could
answer from actual recollection rather than a label.

**User answers, and what they resolved**:
- **`#13` (shared-cluster helm-upgrade policy)**: "helm upgrade can be
  applied, no constraints" — the earlier "no helm upgrade" note was a
  one-off situational call for that session's specific shared-cluster
  conflict, not a standing rule.
- **`#18` (BR-WE-018 root-cause dispute)**: user didn't recall and asked
  for a spike. Found the actual answer in
  `docs/requirements/BR-WE-018-execution-pod-security-hardening.md`
  (added 2026-06-30, closes GAP-03 / issue #1505, Status: Implemented):
  hardcoding `runAsNonRoot` with **no** CRD escape hatch for
  WE-spawned Job/Tekton pods is a **deliberate, documented security
  decision**, not an oversight — the BR's own stated rationale is that
  `WorkflowExecution` specs are AI/LLM-populated, so a configurable
  security-profile field would be an exploitable lever for a compromised
  or malicious workflow/prompt-injection to escape the sandbox. The
  original "critical issue... without proper exception handling" framing
  had it backwards: removing the exception-handling path was the point.
- **`#24`/`#25` (crashloop tape speed)**: "solved a long time ago... it's
  fixed today" — closed both without needing the byte-level tape diff,
  since the user could confirm the current state directly.
- **`#32` (SignalType vs SignalName)**: user asked me to check the live
  CRD fields and recall any clarifying issue. Current
  `RemediationRequest.Spec` keeps **all three** signal fields side by
  side — nothing was removed: `SignalName` (specific, e.g.
  "HighMemoryUsage"), `SignalType` (generic, now always `"alert"`, per
  the code comment "adapter-specific values are deprecated"), and
  `SignalSource` (the ingesting adapter). `AIAnalysis.Status.RootCauseAnalysis.SignalType`
  is likewise still `SignalType`, not renamed — consistent with the
  user's original objection that an RCA doesn't guarantee a signal name.
  The one issue found on this topic, [#166](https://github.com/jordigilh/kubernaut/issues/166)
  ("Eliminate SignalType: rename to SignalSource + IncidentType"), is
  from February 2026 — about 5 months before this dispute and before
  HAPI's Python code was even deleted — and proposes a rename scheme
  that doesn't match what's actually in the code today. It's very likely
  the issue the user was half-remembering, but it's stale/superseded,
  not the source of truth here.
- **`#39` (DCM PR-stacking strategy)**: user confirmed it's a deliberate,
  reasoned tradeoff, not an oversight — DCM PRs require 2 approvals, the
  ~10+ person team is busy building *and* reviewing, and waiting for the
  backlog to clear before stacking wouldn't scale against the team's
  delivery pace. The reviewability concern the team raised is an accepted
  cost of that choice, not a signal to stop.

**Execution**: `/tmp/execute_final_6.py`, same shape as the prior three
passes. All 6 were `invalidate_retain` this time (no `remove_only` — every
entry had a real, keep-worthy resolution). One `invalidate_memory` call hit
the same benign "already gone" (404) result seen throughout today; all 6
`hindsight_retain` calls succeeded. Logged to
`~/.hindsight/logs/final-6-resolution-2026-08-08.jsonl`.

**Queue trajectory for the full day**: 219 → 72 → 48 → 40 → 6 → **0**. The
entire pending-contradictions backlog that existed at the start of today
is now cleared — bulk LLM triage handled the mechanical majority, semantic
clustering + user ground truth handled the judgment calls, targeted
subagent/direct investigation handled the ones code could settle, and a
final round of direct user recall (with full context shown, not just
labels) closed out the handful that no amount of automated investigation
could resolve on its own.

## 2026-08-08 (same day, fifth follow-up): `gopls` Serves Stale Reference Results Because It's a Long-Lived Process on a Live, Branch-Switched Clone — Fixed via `post-checkout`/`post-merge` Auto-Restart

**Context**: the kubernaut team reported `gopls` returning reference-search
content previews (`tools.HandleCreateRR(context.Background(),
&tools.ToolDeps{...}, ...)`, `tools.NewInvestigateMCPTool(&tools.
InvestigateConfig{...}, nil)`) that implied a config-struct refactor not
present in the code they were actually looking at, and called it "out of
sync."

**Root cause, confirmed end-to-end, not inferred**:
1. That config-struct refactor is real — `origin/main`'s
   `pkg/apifrontend/tools/af_create_rr.go`/`agent/root.go` really do have
   `func HandleCreateRR(ctx, d *ToolDeps, args *CreateRRArgs, username
   string)` and `tools.NewInvestigateMCPTool(&tools.InvestigateConfig{...})`,
   from commit `4185bdf17` (2026-06-25). `gopls` wasn't hallucinating a
   phantom signature; it was showing real code from a different commit.
2. The local `kubernaut` clone's `HEAD` was actually on `pr-2000-latest` —
   **1,663 commits behind `main`** — where both functions still had the old
   long-parameter-list signatures with no config structs at all.
3. `git reflog` showed this exact clone was checked out `main →
   pr-2000-latest` the day before, at 2026-08-07 16:46.
4. All three running `gopls mcp` processes (`kubernaut`, `kubernaut-v1.5`,
   `kubernaut-v1.6` — matched PID→workspace via `lsof -p <pid> -d cwd`) had
   been running continuously since **before** that checkout (since Thursday
   3PM), and were never restarted afterward. `kubernaut-v1.5`/`-v1.6` had
   the identical exposure (their own branch switches/rebases the same day),
   confirming this wasn't isolated to the one workspace reported.

`gopls`, run as a long-lived MCP stdio process, does not reliably detect an
out-of-band `git checkout`/`git pull` that rewrites thousands of files at
once and keeps serving results against whatever commit was on disk when it
started. This is the same underlying practice — a live, actively
branch-switched shared clone, rather than a worktree/mirror per branch —
that caused the 2026-08-03 CocoIndex re-ingestion churn above, just
surfacing as a different symptom (stale index vs. redundant re-ingestion)
in a different long-lived process (`gopls` vs. CocoIndex's file watcher).

**Immediate fix**: killed the three stale `gopls mcp` processes (identified
via `lsof -p <pid> -d cwd` to confirm workspace mapping before killing).
Cursor's MCP client auto-respawned fresh ones within seconds via the
existing `cd ${workspaceFolder} && exec gopls mcp` command, which index
whatever is actually on disk now.

**Permanent fix**: there was already a self-healing `post-checkout` git
hook symlinked into 5 kubernaut-family repos
(`kubernaut`/`-v1.5`/`-v1.6`/`-operator`/`-console`), all pointing at one
shared script (`~/.hindsight/git-hooks/post-checkout-cursor-mcp.sh`) that
re-provisions the `.cursor/mcp.json` symlink on every checkout. Extended it
to also kill any running `gopls mcp` process whose `cwd` (via `lsof`)
matches that repo's toplevel — cleanly scoped per-repo, so it can't repeat
the 2026-07-12 "one shared process, one bug kills every window" failure
mode. Added a new shared `_restart-gopls.sh` helper (called from both
hooks) and a new `post-merge` hook (covers `git pull`/fast-forward merges,
which also showed up in the reflog) that self-provisions its own symlink
from `post-checkout` the same way `.cursor/mcp.json` already does, so any
repo that already has the base hook picks up the new one automatically on
its next checkout. Backfilled the `post-merge` symlink into all 5 existing
repos immediately rather than waiting for their next checkout.

**Bonus finding while backfilling**: `kubernaut-operator` has
`core.hooksPath = .githooks`, but its `post-checkout` symlink had only ever
been placed at the now-dead `.git/hooks/post-checkout` — meaning this
whole self-healing mechanism (including the pre-existing `.cursor/mcp.json`
auto-provisioning) had likely never actually fired there. Fixed by
symlinking `post-checkout` into the active `.githooks/` path too.

**Verified via simulated hook invocation** (`sh .git/hooks/post-checkout
HEAD HEAD 1`, no real checkout performed) rather than a real branch switch,
to avoid disturbing any in-progress work in these live dev clones: confirmed
the hook kills only the matching repo's `gopls` process, leaves sibling
workspaces' processes untouched, and exits 0 with `post-merge` already
present (idempotent re-run).

**Known gap, not closed**: stock git has no dedicated `post-rebase` hook,
so a `git rebase` that doesn't also trigger a checkout/merge under the hood
won't restart `gopls`. `kubernaut-v1.6`'s reflog showed exactly this kind
of rebase-only event. Accepted as a residual manual-restart case for now;
`kill $(pgrep -f "gopls mcp")` after a rebase is the workaround until/unless
this is revisited.

**Takeaway**: this is the second time in a week the same root practice —
long-running dev tooling processes bound to a live, frequently
branch-switched shared clone instead of a stable-per-branch worktree —
has caused a real, hard-to-diagnose-from-symptoms-alone bug in a different
tool. Worth keeping in mind for any *future* long-lived process that reads
from these clones' working trees.

## 2026-08-10: Unscoped `reflect()` in `run_nightly()` Started Deterministically Failing as `cursor-memory` Grew — Retired, Not Fixed in Place

**Symptom**: the 2026-08-10 nightly run's `reflect_result` field held
`{"error": "HTTP Error 500: Internal Server Error"}` instead of a
synthesized "top 3 recurring correction patterns" answer.
`reflect_windowed_7d` in the same run succeeded normally (29 corrections in
the 7-day window).

**Initially assumed transient** (matching the pattern of the
2026-07-26/07-28/08-03/08-04 incidents above, all of which were either an
unguarded exception or a genuine network/DNS blip that self-resolved) —
but this one didn't fit: `reflect()`'s own try/except (added after
2026-07-26, see above) was already catching it and returning an error
object exactly as designed, so the run never crashed and nothing was
actually broken in the error-handling sense.

**Confirmed not transient**: manually re-ran `nl.reflect()` against a
`hindsight-api` already confirmed healthy (`{"status":"healthy","database":
"connected"}`) and reproduced the **identical** error:

```
API error 500 http://localhost:8888/v1/default/banks/cursor-memory/reflect: {"detail":"LiteLLM response was truncated due to token limit"}
```

**Root cause**: `reflect()` (nightly-learn.py) posts to `/reflect` with no
date-range parameter at all — as `reflect_windowed()`'s own docstring
already documented when it was added on 2026-08-04 — so every call reasons
over the bank's **entire all-time correction history**. `cursor-memory` had
grown to 12,134 nodes / 4,004 documents / 838,877 links by 2026-08-09, and
that history is now large enough that the underlying LiteLLM call truncates
before finishing, which `hindsight-api` surfaces as an HTTP 500. This isn't
a one-off blip: the bank only grows, so the identical failure will recur
every night going forward with zero chance of self-resolving.

**Why fix-in-place (retry/backoff) doesn't apply here**: every prior
`reflect()` incident in this file was fixed by either adding error handling
(2026-07-26) or waiting for a transient outage to clear and backfilling the
one missed night (2026-07-28, 2026-08-03/04). Neither applies to a
deterministic failure — retrying produces the same 500 every time, and
there is nothing to backfill 2026-08-10.json with, so it was left showing
the real error rather than a synthetic result.

**Decision — retire the call, don't fix it**: `reflect_result` (the
unscoped call's output) was never actually read by anything downstream —
`generate-dashboard.py` only ever renders `reflect_windowed_7d` (see its
own comment, added 2026-08-04, calling the unscoped variant out as barely
changing night to night even after a pattern stops recurring). Given (a) no
consumer depends on it, (b) the bounded, sustainable replacement
(`reflect_windowed()`, added 2026-08-04 for exactly this scalability
concern, capped at 40 correction facts client-side regardless of bank size)
already exists and already succeeded that same night, and (c) the unscoped
call cannot be made to work again without bounding its input the same way,
`run_nightly()` no longer calls the unscoped `reflect()` at all.
`results["reflect_result"]` stays at its initialized `None` for schema
compatibility with historical JSON logs; the `reflect()` function itself
is left in place (unused by the nightly pipeline, still available for any
ad-hoc/manual invocation).

**TDD**: added `TestRunNightlyReflectRetired` to `tests/test_nightly_learn.py`
— confirmed RED first (asserted `reflect()` is never called and
`reflect_result` stays `None`; both failed against the pre-fix code, one
by actually invoking the injected-to-fail stub), then GREEN after removing
the `results["reflect_result"] = reflect()` line. All 82 tests in the file
pass; `tests/test_generate_dashboard.py` unaffected.

---

## 2026-08-10 (same day, follow-up): Kubernaut Code Search Went Multi-Branch — `release/v1.5`/`release/v1.6` Mirrors Indexed, With a Directory-Name Fallback for the Real Feature-Branch Workflow the Literal `release/vX.Y` Check Missed

**Trigger**: the user noticed `cocoindex_search` from a `release/v1.5`
workspace was returning `main`'s code, not v1.5's — a direct consequence of
the 2026-08-03 fix above (`Runaway CocoIndex Re-ingestion...`), which
deliberately mirrored only `main` to stop branch-switching churn on the
*docs* pipeline, but had the side effect of making *code* search
branch-blind too, even though code embedding (unlike docs) doesn't call
`hindsight_retain()` and so was never actually subject to that churn/cost
concern in the first place.

**Convention adopted**: index `main` plus a small, proactively-chosen set of
release lines (`release/v1.5`, `release/v1.6` — the next one, added ahead of
the branch actually existing yet) for the whole kubernaut family (`kubernaut`,
`kubernaut-operator`, `kubernaut-console`); leave every other branch
(feature/fix work) to Serena, which reads the live checkout directly and
needs no index at all.

**Shipped**:
- `watch-mirrors-config.sh`/`watch-mirrors-lib.sh`: added
  `RELEASE_LINES`/`RELEASE_WATCH_MIRRORS` (6 entries: 3 repos × 2 lines) and
  made `ensure_mirror()` check upstream branch existence via `git ls-remote`
  first, logging INFO (not ERROR) and skipping cleanly for a line that isn't
  cut yet — confirmed live: `release/v1.6` doesn't exist on any of the 3
  repos yet, mirrors created cleanly for `release/v1.5` on all 3, `v1.6`
  skipped without noise on all 3.
- `cocoindex-flows.py`'s `code_main`: loops repo × release-line over the new
  mirrors, tagging `repo_tag="{repo}@release-{line}"` — code embedding has no
  `hindsight_retain()` call, so this adds zero LLM-consolidation cost, only
  extra embedding/BM25-index compute. Backfilled: 1,023 rows added, 1,152
  reprocessed across the 6 new mirror trees (main's existing rows untouched).
- `cocoindex-search.py`: new `branch` param on both `search_code()`/
  `pattern_search_code()` (and their `cocoindex_search`/
  `cocoindex_pattern_search` MCP tool wrappers + CLI flags), resolved by
  `_resolve_release_line()` — explicit value wins, otherwise
  `_detect_current_release_line()` auto-detects. **Default (main/undetected)
  behavior explicitly excludes every `@`-tagged row** (`NOT LIKE '%@%'`) so
  release content can never silently leak into today's baseline results —
  this was the single most important invariant to get right, since a bug
  here would recreate the exact "wrong branch's code" symptom that motivated
  this whole feature.
- `mcp.json` templates (`kubernaut-family-go.json`,
  `kubernaut-family-nogo.json`) + `io.vectorize.cocoindex.service.plist`:
  added `KUBERNAUT_LIVE_CLONE_DIR` (`${workspaceFolder}`) and
  `KUBERNAUT_RELEASE_LINES` respectively so the search server can see both
  which lines exist and which checkout it's running alongside. Found (and
  fixed) `kubernaut-console/.cursor/mcp.json` was a plain-file copy of the
  nogo template rather than a symlink — converted it, removing a drift risk
  this change would otherwise have silently inherited.

**Real-world gap found during verification, fixed before shipping**: the
literal `release/v(.+)$` branch-name check almost never fires in practice —
checking all 5 live kubernaut-family clones found **zero** actually checked
out on a literal `release/*` branch; all 5 were on feature/fix branches
(`fix/2086-workflow-discovery-hang`, `port/2071-2073-2075-2080-to-main`,
etc.). This team's real workflow is a **dedicated clone directory per release
line** (`kubernaut-v1.5`, `kubernaut-v1.6` alongside plain `kubernaut` for
`main`), with feature work branched *inside* the matching directory rather
than worked on directly on the release branch. Confirmed
`kubernaut-v1.5`'s checked-out `fix/2086-...` branch has merge-base ==
`origin/release/v1.5` HEAD (0 commits ahead) vs. 269 commits ahead of
`origin/main` — it really is v1.5-line work, just never literally *on* that
branch. Added a second detection tier to `_detect_current_release_line()`:
if the branch name doesn't match, fall back to a `-v(\d+\.\d+)$` suffix on
the clone directory's own basename. Cheap (no network, no merge-base walk —
just a path-string regex) and matches the convention exactly; the literal
branch-name check still takes priority when someone genuinely is on
`release/vX.Y`.

**Verification**: spot-checked `cocoindex_search`/`cocoindex_pattern_search`
end-to-end (not just unit tests) against the real `kubernaut`/`kubernaut-v1.5`
clones — `KUBERNAUT_LIVE_CLONE_DIR` pointed at each in turn reliably returned
`kubernaut/...` vs. `kubernaut@release-v1.5/...` filepaths for the identical
query. Killed the 3 stale `cocoindex-search.py` MCP server processes so
Cursor respawns them with the new code on next tool call.

**TDD**: `tests/test_cocoindex_flows.py::TestReleaseLineWiring` (release-line
dir convention + `code_main` source coverage) and the new
`tests/test_cocoindex_search.py` (32 tests: detection incl. both tiers +
priority ordering, resolution, SQL WHERE-clause construction, pattern-root
selection, and `search_code()`'s actual SQL/params via a fake psycopg2
connection) — all passing alongside the full existing suite.

## 2026-08-11: Koku PR/Jira Ingestion Capped to the 2000 Most Recent — 5+ Years of History Was Diluting Relevance for Temporal Contributors

**Trigger**: mid-backfill (see the 2026-08-10 koku onboarding entries above),
the user requested a scope change: "for koku we should just ingest the last
2000 PRs, the project is 5 years old and things have changed substantially in
the code that I don't think older issues are any relevant to our tasks (we
are temporal contributors)." Asked whether the same policy should extend to
koku's actual ticket tracker (Jira, project COST, ~7,800 issues), the user
confirmed yes, apply it there too.

**Mechanism, not judgment**: both cuts are pure recency cutoffs on each
source API's own ordering — no LLM is involved in deciding what's "relevant"
to keep. `gh pr list` returns newest-created-first by default (verified live:
`--limit 5` returned PR #6258 down to #6254, each with a strictly decreasing
`createdAt`), so `--limit KOKU_PR_LIMIT` (env-configurable, default 2000) is
a straightforward newest-N cutoff. The `_fetch_all_jira_issues()` JQL sort
was changed from `order by key asc` to `order by created desc`, and
pagination now stops as soon as `KOKU_JIRA_LIMIT` (default 2000) is reached
(`while len(all_items) < limit`, final `all_items[:limit]` guard) instead of
walking every page unconditionally. The only LLM step anywhere in this
pipeline is the pre-existing downstream `hindsight_retain()` consolidation
call, unchanged in mechanism — it now simply runs over fewer, more-recent
items.

**Applied narrowly to koku only** — consistent with an earlier same-day
correction on a different project ("changes should apply only to DCM repos,
not kubernaut repos"): `KOKU_PR_LIMIT`/`KOKU_JIRA_LIMIT` are new constants
local to `koku-cocoindex-flows.py`, with no equivalent cap added to
kubernaut's, praxis's, or engram's own flows.

**Mid-flight correction**: the in-progress koku backfill had already fetched
all 5,363 PRs under the old unlimited `--limit 10000` and was mid-consolidation
(one of `hindsight-api`'s 2 shared consolidation slots) when the new
constraint landed. Killed and restarted it rather than letting it finish
under the stale policy — letting it complete would have spent real LLM
consolidation cost on PRs about to be out of scope anyway. Restarting is safe
and non-wasteful here: `process_pr`/`process_jira_issue` are `@coco.fn(memo=True)`,
so any of the ~2000-in-scope items already retained before the kill are
skipped as memo cache hits on the rerun, not redone.

**Not addressed (deliberately, awaiting a separate decision)**: any PRs/Jira
issues outside the new window that were already retained into the
`koku-issues` bank by earlier (pre-2026-08-11) backfill runs remain in
Hindsight — this change only affects what future ingestion fetches, it is
not a retroactive purge. `docs/FINDINGS.md` mentions this as an open
question if the user later wants stale-history cleanup too.

**Verification**: restarted the backfill under
`with-config-env.sh koku-cocoindex-flows.py --mode backfill --apps docs
issues code`; confirmed via live log output that `project-koku/koku` PR
fetch stopped at exactly 2000 (vs. 5,363 previously) and
`koku-service-operator` returned 26 (well under the cap — a newer, smaller
repo where the limit is a no-op, as expected), and Jira COST pagination
stopped at exactly 2000 across 20 pages of 100 rather than continuing toward
~7,800.

**Operational note, not a code bug**: the first restart attempt was lost
almost immediately after backgrounding via a bare shell `&` (no
`block_until_ms: 0`) — the same premature-termination gotcha already on
record from the 2026-08-10 praxis backfill relaunch. Re-launched correctly
the second time; flagging the recurrence since it's now bitten twice in two
days and is worth remembering as a standing gotcha for this kind of
long-running background job.

**TDD**: `tests/test_koku_cocoindex_flows.py` — `test_pr_limit_defaults_to_2000`,
`TestFetchAllPrs::test_gh_pr_list_invoked_with_configured_limit` (asserts the
constructed `gh` command's `--limit` argument), and
`TestFetchAllJiraIssues` (`test_default_limit_is_2000`,
`test_jql_sorts_by_created_desc_for_recency`,
`test_stops_paginating_once_limit_reached` — a synthetic 2-page fixture
confirming pagination halts mid-page-2 once the cap is hit rather than
draining every page first and truncating after) — all passing alongside the
full existing suite (527 tests).

---

## 2026-08-11 (same day, follow-up): Serena vs. Cursor's Built-In Tools — Decided to Keep Serena, on Cross-Language Consistency Rather Than Raw Capability

**Trigger**: user read a Cursor community forum thread ("In Cursor, is there
really a need for Serena/Sequential Thinking/Exa/Context7 these types of
MCP", forum.cursor.com/t/149157) ahead of a discussion with a colleague about
whether Serena is still worth keeping now that Cursor's native agent
capabilities have grown. Cursor staff's own answer in that thread (Dean Rie,
post #4): built-in semantic search, web search, browser automation, and
file/terminal tools cover a lot, but "Serena (code understanding) is
**partly** covered by semantic search" — not fully — and the practical
recommendation was to try detaching MCP servers and see what the agent can't
do on its own.

**Decision: keep Serena.** The deciding factor the user settled on was not
raw capability (semantic search vs. LSP) but **cross-language consistency**:
Serena exposes one MCP tool surface
(`find_symbol`/`find_referencing_symbols`/`get_diagnostics_for_file`) across
every language this project has onboarded (`gopls` for Go, `pyright` for
Python, `rust-analyzer` for Rust, `typescript-language-server` for
TypeScript — see `docs/NEW_PROJECT_SETUP.md`), rather than requiring a
bespoke raw-LSP-MCP integration (and a bespoke rule-template section) per
language. This matters specifically because this repo maintains shared rule
templates (`cursor/hindsight-memory.mdc.tmpl`) synced across a multi-language
project family (kubernaut=Go, koku=Python/Django, console=TypeScript,
dcm/praxis/engram=Python) — one tool vocabulary that works everywhere is a
maintainability win a single-language project wouldn't get as much value
from. Serena's `solidlsp` layer also auto-manages the underlying LSP binary
per language (no manual `gopls`/`pyright` install), which a hand-rolled
per-language raw LSP MCP wouldn't give for free either.

**This wasn't decided from a cold start — it's consistent with two verdicts
already on record in this file.** The 2026-08-07 (fifth follow-up) entry
above already tested "can Cursor-native/structural search replace
gopls/Serena" for both Go (kubernaut) and Python (koku, where Serena was
onboarded 2026-07-31 specifically as a Pyright-backed `gopls`-equivalent)
and got "no" both times: `find_symbol`/`find_referencing_symbols`/
diagnostics are type-aware, cross-file-accurate capabilities that neither
embedding-based semantic search nor `CodePattern`'s structural shape-matching
can replicate. So the real choice was never "Serena vs. nothing" — it was
"one consistent LSP frontend vs. N per-language ad-hoc ones."

**One counter-argument pre-empted for the colleague discussion**: MCP tool
schemas cost context tokens on every turn, which sounds like a reason to
detach a large MCP server like Serena when idle. Cursor staff (Kevin
Neilson) clarified in a separate thread
(forum.cursor.com/t/166405, reply #5) that Cursor does **dynamic context
discovery** — it injects a compact catalog of attached servers/tool names,
not every tool's full schema, loading the full description/input-schema
only when the agent actually needs it — citing a **46.9% reduction in agent
tokens** from this change in tool-using runs. So "detach unused MCP servers
to save tokens every turn" is a weaker argument against keeping Serena
attached than it would have been under the old always-inject-everything
behavior.

**Status, not policy**: per the existing `.gitignore` comment convention
(`# Serena MCP trial (project config/cache/logs; not yet a team decision)`),
this remains a personal-trial adoption, not a team-wide mandate — this entry
records the reasoning behind keeping it for the *next* project onboarded,
not a retroactive claim that every current adopter re-justified it today.

---

## 2026-08-11 (same day, second follow-up): kubernaut's Live CocoIndex Ingestion Was Down ~6 Hours — Deployed `plist` Had Drifted Behind the Repo Template

**Trigger**: the status-report canvas above flagged `io.vectorize.cocoindex.service`
(kubernaut's live docs/code/issues/transcripts watcher) as not running and not
loaded in launchd at all; asked to address it directly.

**What happened**: `cocoindex-stderr.log` shows a `code-app crashed` event at
2026-08-10 23:56:32, preceded by ~90 minutes of repeated `Issues poll error:
timed out` and `component build failed: TimeoutError` entries. The process
then ran cleanly for another ~3 hours (issues polling every 5 minutes as
normal) before going silent at 02:56:50 with no further log lines besides a
benign multiprocessing semaphore-cleanup warning — no explicit shutdown or
crash trace at that final stop, so exactly what killed the process at ~03:00
isn't confirmed from the log alone. `hindsight-api`'s own logs show no errors
at 23:56, ruling out a `hindsight-api` outage as the trigger for the initial
crash.

**Real gap found — not the same thing as the crash, but likely contributed to
it not recovering**: the deployed `~/Library/LaunchAgents/
io.vectorize.cocoindex.service.plist` (regenerated 2026-08-10 22:16,
incidentally the same batch as unrelated praxis/`report.py` work) had drifted
behind this repo's canonical `launchd/io.vectorize.cocoindex.service.plist` —
missing two env vars added by prior, already-shipped fixes:
`COCOINDEX_PG_POOL_MIN_SIZE`/`MAX_SIZE` (2026-08-03's fix for the
default-10-connection Postgres pool every `*-cocoindex-flows.py` was holding
unnecessarily) and `KUBERNAUT_RELEASE_LINES` (2026-08-10's multi-branch code
search feature, which makes `code_main` loop repo × release-line — more
concurrent connection pressure against the same shared Postgres instance than
before that feature existed). So the instance that crashed was running
without the pool-size cap in place. This is circumstantial, not a proven root
cause of the `TimeoutError`s — flagging it as the leading suspect, not a
confirmed diagnosis.

**Fix**: redeployed via the documented step (`docs/INSTALL.md` §16:
`sed "s|__HOME__|$HOME|g" launchd/io.vectorize.cocoindex.service.plist >
~/Library/LaunchAgents/io.vectorize.cocoindex.service.plist`, then
`launchctl load`). Verified clean: all 4 apps (docs/code/issues/transcripts)
logged `Starting <app>...`, the first issues-poll cycle completed with no
errors (`Issues poll: complete, next in 300s`), and the process stayed up
afterward. `KeepAlive`/`RunAtLoad` are both `true` in the plist, so it will
now auto-restart on crash and on reboot going forward, unlike its state
during the ~6-hour gap.

**Not yet done — flagged, not actioned this session**: didn't diagnose *why*
the deployed plist regenerated to a stale version in the first place (no
script owns that copy step today; it looks like a manual `sed`/`cp` from an
older checkout), and didn't check whether the other projects' deployed
plists (`dcm`, `koku`, `praxis`, `engram`) have the same kind of drift —
worth a follow-up sweep since this could silently reintroduce old,
already-fixed bugs on any of them.

---

## 2026-08-11 (same day, third follow-up): Swept dcm/koku/praxis/engram Deployed Plists for the Same Drift — Found and Fixed a Second Instance, in Engram's Own Live Ingestion

**Trigger**: direct follow-up to the kubernaut plist-drift fix above — swept the
remaining four projects' deployed `~/Library/LaunchAgents/io.vectorize.cocoindex.*.plist`
files against their repo templates (`launchd/io.vectorize.cocoindex.*.plist`,
`__HOME__`-substituted) to check for the same kind of drift.

**Results**:
- **dcm**: deployed plist matches the repo template exactly. No drift.
- **koku**: no deployed plist at all (`~/Library/LaunchAgents` has no entry) — not
 drift, this project hasn't been promoted to a continuous `launchctl`-managed live
 service yet; it's still manual-backfill-only (consistent with the 24h status report
 above, which found koku had a scope change, not a live-mode onboarding).
- **praxis**: same as koku — no deployed plist yet. Newly onboarded this session,
 backfill-only so far, live promotion not done.
- **engram: drift found — same gap as kubernaut, in this project's own ingestion.**
 The deployed `io.vectorize.cocoindex.engram.plist` was missing the 2026-08-03
 `COCOINDEX_PG_POOL_MIN_SIZE`/`MAX_SIZE` pair (right-sizing `code_app`'s pgvector
 pool down from asyncpg's own `min_size=10`/`max_size=10` default). Unlike
 kubernaut, this hadn't caused a visible crash — the process had been running
 continuously and healthily (0 errors in 7+ days per the status report above) — so
 this was a latent risk rather than an active incident: engram's own `code_app` was
 quietly holding up to 10 unnecessary Postgres connections against the same shared
 instance every other project's `cocoindex-flows.py` pools against, same as
 kubernaut had been before 08-03's fix, just without (yet) tripping a timeout.
 Notable because this is the plist backing the `cocoindex_search`/
 `engram_code_search` MCP tools used in this very session.

**Fix**: `launchctl unload` the running service → redeploy via the same documented
step as kubernaut (`sed "s|__HOME__|$HOME|g" launchd/io.vectorize.cocoindex.engram.plist
> ~/Library/LaunchAgents/io.vectorize.cocoindex.engram.plist`) → `launchctl load`.
Verified: diff against the repo template is now empty, both apps (`code`, `docs`)
logged `Starting <app>...` on the fresh load, and `Hindsight API healthy` confirmed
on restart.

**Still open**: the root cause of *why* deployed plists drift at all — no script
owns the repo-template → `~/Library/LaunchAgents` copy step; every fix so far
(kubernaut, engram) has been a manual `sed`/`launchctl load` cited from
`docs/INSTALL.md` §16, with nothing preventing the same drift from recurring the
next time either plist's source template changes. A cheap follow-up would be a
small idempotent script (or a `Makefile`/`justfile` target) that redeploys all
five plists from their templates in one pass, callable after any env-var change to
a template — turning this from a remembered manual step into a repeatable one.
